Symptom fixes are failure.\n\n**Violating the letter of this process is violating the spirit of debugging.**\n\n## The Iron Law\n\n```\nNO FIXES WITHOUT ROOT CAUSE INVESTIGATION FIRST\n```\n\nIf you haven't completed Phase 1, you cannot propose fixes.\n\n## When to Use\n\nUse for ANY technical issue:\n- Test failures\n- Bugs in production\n- Unexpected behavior\n- Performance problems\n- Build failures\n- Integration issues\n\n**Use this ESPECIALLY when:**\n- Under time pressure (emergencies make guessing tempting)\n- \"Just one quick fix\" seems obvious\n- You've already tried multiple fixes\n- Previous fix didn't work\n- You don't fully understand the issue\n\n**Don't skip when:**\n- Issue seems simple (simple bugs have root causes too)\n- You're in a hurry (rushing guarantees rework)\n- Manager wants it fixed NOW (systematic is faster than thrashing)\n\n## The Four Phases\n\nYou MUST complete each phase before proceeding to the next.\n\n### Phase 1: Root Cause Investigation\n\n**BEFORE attempting ANY fix:**\n\n1. **Read Error Messages Carefully**\n   - Don't skip past errors or warnings\n   - They often contain the exact solution\n   - Read stack traces completely\n   - Note line numbers, file paths, error codes\n\n2. **Reproduce Consistently**\n   - Can you trigger it reliably?\n   - What are the exact steps?\n   - Does it happen every time?\n   - If not reproducible → gather more data, don't guess\n\n3. **Check Recent Changes**\n   - What changed that could cause this?\n   - Git diff, recent commits\n   - New dependencies, config changes\n   - Environmental differences\n\n4. **Gather Evidence in Multi-Component Systems**\n\n   **WHEN system has multiple components (CI → build → signing, API → service → database):**\n\n   **BEFORE proposing fixes, add diagnostic instrumentation:**\n   ```\n   For EACH component boundary:\n     - Log what data enters component\n     - Log what data exits component\n     - Verify environment/config propagation\n     - Check state at each layer\n\n   Run once to gather evidence showing WHERE it breaks\n   THEN analyze evidence to identify failing component\n   THEN investigate that specific component\n   ```\n\n   **Example (multi-layer system):**\n   ```bash\n   # Layer 1: Workflow\n   echo \"=== Secrets available in workflow: ===\"\n   echo \"IDENTITY: ${IDENTITY:+SET}${IDENTITY:-UNSET}\"\n\n   # Layer 2: Build script\n   echo \"=== Env vars in build script: ===\"\n   env | grep IDENTITY || echo \"IDENTITY not in environment\"\n\n   # Layer 3: Signing script\n   echo \"=== Keychain state: ===\"\n   security list-keychains\n   security find-identity -v\n\n   # Layer 4: Actual signing\n   codesign --sign \"$IDENTITY\" --verbose=4 \"$APP\"\n   ```\n\n   **This reveals:** Which layer fails (secrets → workflow ✓, workflow → build ✗)\n\n5. **Trace Data Flow**\n\n   **WHEN error is deep in call stack:**\n\n   See `root-cause-tracing.md` in this directory for the complete backward tracing technique.\n\n   **Quick version:**\n   - Where does bad value originate?\n   - What called this with bad value?\n   - Keep tracing up until you find the source\n   - Fix at source, not at symptom\n\n### Phase 2: Pattern Analysis\n\n**Find the pattern before fixing:**\n\n1. **Find Working Examples**\n   - Locate similar working code in same codebase\n   - What works that's similar to what's broken?\n\n2. **Compare Against References**\n   - If implementing pattern, read reference implementation COMPLETELY\n   - Don't skim - read every line\n   - Understand the pattern fully before applying\n\n3. **Identify Differences**\n   - What's different between working and broken?\n   - List every difference, however small\n   - Don't assume \"that can't matter\"\n\n4. **Understand Dependencies**\n   - What other components does this need?\n   - What settings, config, environment?\n   - What assumptions does it make?\n\n### Phase 3: Hypothesis and Testing\n\n**Scientific method:**\n\n1. **Form Single Hypothesis**\n   - State clearly: \"I think X is the root cause because Y\"\n   - Write it down\n   - Be specific, not vague\n\n2. **Test Minimally**\n   - Make the SMALLEST possible change to test hypothesis\n   - One variable at a time\n   - Don't fix multiple things at once\n\n3. **Verify Before Continuing**\n   - Did it work? Yes → Phase 4\n   - Didn't work? Form NEW hypothesis\n   - DON'T add more fixes on top\n\n4. **When You Don't Know**\n   - Say \"I don't understand X\"\n   - Don't pretend to know\n   - Ask for help\n   - Research more\n\n### Phase 4: Implementation\n\n**Fix the root cause, not the symptom:**\n\n1. **Create Failing Test Case**\n   - Simplest possible reproduction\n   - Automated test if possible\n   - One-off test script if no framework\n   - MUST have before fixing\n   - Use the `ultrapowers:test-driven-development` skill for writing proper failing tests\n\n2. **Implement Single Fix**\n   - Address the root cause identified\n   - ONE change at a time\n   - No \"while I'm here\" improvements\n   - No bundled refactoring\n\n3. **Verify Fix**\n   - Test passes now?\n   - No other tests broken?\n   - Issue actually resolved?\n   - If the bug was meaningful or systemic, use `ultrapowers:learning-from-bugs` before declaring the fix complete.\n\n4. **If Fix Doesn't Work**\n   - STOP\n   - Count: How many fixes have you tried?\n   - If < 3: Return to Phase 1, re-analyze with new information\n   - **If ≥ 3: STOP and question the architecture (step 5 below)**\n   - DON'T attempt Fix #4 without architectural discussion\n\n5. **If 3+ Fixes Failed: Question Architecture**\n\n   **Pattern indicating architectural problem:**\n   - Each fix reveals new shared state/coupling/problem in different place\n   - Fixes require \"massive refactoring\" to implement\n   - Each fix creates new symptoms elsewhere\n\n   **STOP and question fundamentals:**\n   - Is this pattern fundamentally sound?\n   - Are we \"sticking with it through sheer inertia\"?\n   - Should we refactor architecture vs. continue fixing symptoms?\n\n   **Discuss with your human partner before attempting more fixes**\n\n   This is NOT a failed hypothesis - this is a wrong architecture.\n\n## Red Flags - STOP and Follow Process\n\nIf you catch yourself thinking:\n- \"Quick fix for now, investigate later\"\n- \"Just try changing X and see if it works\"\n- \"Add multiple changes, run tests\"\n- \"Skip the test, I'll manually verify\"\n- \"It's probably X, let me fix that\"\n- \"I don't fully understand but this might work\"\n- \"Pattern says X but I'll adapt it differently\"\n- \"Here are the main problems: [lists fixes without investigation]\"\n- Proposing solutions before tracing data flow\n- **\"One more fix attempt\" (when already tried 2+)**\n- **Each fix reveals new problem in different place**\n\n**ALL of these mean: STOP. Return to Phase 1.**\n\n**If 3+ fixes failed:** Question the architecture (see Phase 4.5)\n\n## your human partner's Signals You're Doing It Wrong\n\n**Watch for these redirections:**\n- \"Is that not happening?\" - You assumed without verifying\n- \"Will it show us...?\" - You should have added evidence gathering\n- \"Stop guessing\" - You're proposing fixes without understanding\n- \"Ultrathink this\" - Question fundamentals, not just symptoms\n- \"We're stuck?\" (frustrated) - Your approach isn't working\n\n**When you see these:** STOP. Return to Phase 1.\n\n## Common Rationalizations\n\n| Excuse | Reality |\n|--------|---------|\n| \"Issue is simple, don't need process\" | Simple issues have root causes too. Process is fast for simple bugs. |\n| \"Emergency, no time for process\" | Systematic debugging is FASTER than guess-and-check thrashing. |\n| \"Just try this first, then investigate\" | First fix sets the pattern. Do it right from the start. |\n| \"I'll write test after confirming fix works\" | Untested fixes don't stick. Test first proves it. |\n| \"Multiple fixes at once saves time\" | Can't isolate what worked. Causes new bugs. |\n| \"Reference too long, I'll adapt the pattern\" | Partial understanding guarantees bugs. Read it completely. |\n| \"I see the problem, let me fix it\" | Seeing symptoms ≠ understanding root cause. |\n| \"One more fix attempt\" (after 2+ failures) | 3+ failures = architectural problem. Question pattern, don't fix again. |\n\n## Quick Reference\n\n| Phase | Key Activities | Success Criteria |\n|-------|---------------|------------------|\n| **1. Root Cause** | Read errors, reproduce, check changes, gather evidence | Understand WHAT and WHY |\n| **2. Pattern** | Find working examples, compare | Identify differences |\n| **3. Hypothesis** | Form theory, test minimally | Confirmed or new hypothesis |\n| **4. Implementation** | Create test, fix, verify | Bug resolved, tests pass |\n\n## When Process Reveals \"No Root Cause\"\n\nIf systematic investigation reveals issue is truly environmental, timing-dependent, or external:\n\n1. You've completed the process\n2. Document what you investigated\n3. Implement appropriate handling (retry, timeout, error message)\n4. Add monitoring/logging for future investigation\n\n**But:** 95% of \"no root cause\" cases are incomplete investigation.\n\n## Supporting Techniques\n\n---\nname: instrumenting-control-planes\ndescription: Use when building, planning, reviewing, or debugging apps with backend decisions, agent workflows, long-running jobs, SSE/WebSocket streams, recovery paths, deletes, retries, or user-visible failures\n---\n\n# Instrumenting Control Planes\n\nBuild the control plane as a product surface. If the server decides, refuses, retries, spawns, deletes, recovers, gives up, or fails, that decision must be observable by users, operators, and tests.\n\nIf a React UI consumes the control plane through server state, optimistic UI, caches, polling, SSE/WebSocket streams, or persisted runtime state, also use `client-server-state-invariants`.\n\n## Core Rule\n\nEvery meaningful server-side state transition emits a named, durable event. The UI and tests consume the same production event surface.\n\nDo not rely on snapshot diffs, rendered DOM state, logs only, or client-side re-derivation to prove what the server decided.\n\n## Hot Path Rule\n\nNo user bootstrap, catalog, status, reconnect, or recovery request may depend on a lock-prone database write path or external health probe finishing. Serve these paths from a bounded read model, memory cache, append-only event log, or stale-but-marked snapshot; queue reconciliation writes outside the request. Every dependency on a database or bridge call in a hot path needs a short timeout, cancellation, and an explicit degraded response.\n\nIf SQLite, local files, or a single-writer store is used, assume writer contention can freeze readers at the worst moment. Use one writer, WAL/busy-timeout discipline, short transactions, bounded queues, and headless chaos tests that run bootstrap/reload while writes are active. A 25s UI request is a control-plane failure, even if the database eventually returns.\n\n## Event Contract\n\nFor each backend or agent workflow, define:\n\n- named events for decisions: `worker.spawned`, `worker.reattached`, `plan.ready`, `plan.review.blocked`, `recovery.gave_up`, `conversation.delete_failed`;\n- payloads with stable ids, `prev`/`next` status when relevant, reason codes, and enough context to debug;\n- `error.surfaced` or an equivalent user-facing failure event for every failure the user should know about;\n- durable persistence or a bounded replay buffer so short disconnects do not erase history;\n- a scriptable read path: CLI, HTTP endpoint, sqlite query, or event log command.\n\nIf a decision cannot be asserted from this surface, the design is incomplete.\n\nIf debugging a control-plane failure reveals a reusable lesson, use `ultrapowers:learning-from-bugs` after the fix is verified.\n\n## Streaming Requirements\n\nFor SSE or WebSocket-like streams:\n\n- emit named events alongside any snapshot/update frame;\n- include monotonic event ids;\n- support resume from the last seen id;\n- replay missed events from a bounded buffer;\n- emit an explicit resync-required event when replay is impossible;\n- bootstrap from a snapshot, then tail events.\n\nSnapshots show current truth. Named events show why it became true.\n\n## Testing Standard\n\nControl-plane tests should usually be headless HTTP/SSE scenario tests, not browser tests. Browser automation is for UI behavior; control-plane tests assert server decisions directly.\n\nWrite scenario assertions as event transcripts:\n\n```ts\nexpect(events).toHaveEmitted(\"worker.spawned\", { runId });\nexpect(events).toHaveEmitted(\"worker.status\", { next: \"running\" });\nchaos.killServer();\nchaos.restartServer();\nexpect(events).toHaveEmitted(\"worker.reattached\", { runId });\nexpect(events).not.toHaveEmitted(\"worker.recreated\", { runId });\n```\n\nChaos belongs outside production code paths: drop connections in the client, fail fetches in the harness, restart the server process, and use seeds for reproducibility.\n\n## Refuse These Shapes\n\n- silent early returns in server methods;\n- swallowed `catch` blocks;\n- client-side derivation of server decisions;\n- response-code-only user failures;\n- event streams without ids or resume;\n- tests that infer control-plane behavior from rendered state;\n- delete handlers that do not enumerate dependent rows or emit typed failure events;\n- recovery loops without terminal events such as `gave_up` or `abandoned`;\n- request handlers that wait on inline reconciliation writes, SQLite contention, or bridge `/doctor` checks before returning bootstrap data;\n- test-only behavior branches in production code.\n\n## Design Checklist\n\nBefore implementing a backend or agent workflow, answer:\n\n1. What decisions can this workflow make?\n2. What named event is emitted for each decision, including refusal and failure?\n3. Where are events persisted or buffered?\n4. How does a client resume after disconnect?\n5. Which request paths must keep working while persistence or bridge dependencies are slow, locked, or stale?\n6. How can a CLI or test harness inspect the exact event sequence without the UI?\n7. Which headless scenario proves the happy path, failure path, recovery path, terminal state, and hot-path timeout/degraded path?\n8. Which client state consumes these events, and how does it prove ownership, freshness, completeness, ordering, and replay/resync?\n\nIf the answer is \"the UI can figure it out\", name the missing event instead.\n"

    "text": "---\nname: client-server-state-invariants\ndescription: Use when planning, building, reviewing, or debugging React apps with server state, optimistic UI, caches, polling, SSE/WebSocket streams, background jobs, persistence, or client/server synchronization\n---\n\n# Client Server State Invariants\n\nReact bugs often expose deeper ownership mistakes. When client state, server state, cache state, stream state, and persisted state all exist, every plan needs explicit invariants.\n\n## Core Rule\n\nNo UI surface may treat data as authoritative unless the payload proves ownership, freshness, completeness, ordering, and terminal state.\n\nIf those words are missing from the plan, the plan is probably baking in a race condition.\n\n## Planning Checklist\n\nFor every React app that reads server/runtime state, the plan must answer:\n\n1. **Owner:** What owns this state: server, client manager, URL, cache, stream, local storage, or worker/runtime?\n2. **Token:** What owner token protects async results: `requestId`, `runId`, `projectPath`, `operationId`, `workerId + seq`, or equivalent?\n3. **Provenance:** Is data server-authoritative, optimistic, cached preview, partial update, or historical fallback?\n4. **Completeness:** Does the payload declare what scope it is complete for? What may it erase?\n5. **Ordering:** What stable order is used: monotonic seq, `(createdAt, id)`, version, or cursor?\n6. **State machine:** What are the legal states, terminal states, and transitions?\n7. **Events:** Which server decisions are named events rather than client-side derivations?\n8. **Loading shape:** Which states are bootstrap, first load, incremental refresh, reconnect, resync, recovery, and terminal error?\n9. **Fallbacks:** Which legacy/fallback rows can render, and only after what authority is loaded?\n10. **Tests:** Which race, stale-response, event-sequence, reconnect, and merge tests prove the invariants?\n\n## Invariants To Require\n\n- Cached and optimistic data may pre-render, but cannot satisfy full-load gates.\n- Partial snapshots may add or refine state, but may not erase optimistic objects, delivered user input, read markers, or stream entries unless they declare complete scope.\n- Every async response checks that its owner token still matches before mutating visible state.\n- Mutation success handlers may navigate or clear UI only if they still own the current context, unless the action explicitly creates a new selected context.\n- Timers and polling callbacks re-check ownership immediately before acting.\n- Server decisions are emitted as named events; the client does not infer decisions by diffing snapshots.\n- SSE/WebSocket streams have event ids, replay/resume, and an explicit resync path.\n- Loading booleans are split by lifecycle stage. A leaf stream refresh must not unmount the whole page.\n- Append-only content has one writer, one reader, stable ids, and monotonic cursors.\n- Fallback DB rows or historical rows are gated behind stream loading and deduped by stable id plus safe equivalence.\n- Status classifiers distinguish active, runnable, blocked, awaiting user, terminal, stale, and recoverable states.\n- \"Latest\" queries have deterministic tie-breakers. Timestamp-only ordering is not enough.\n- Hot paths use bounded reads and do not synchronously load large bodies, bridge probes, schema setup, or heavy app graphs.\n- Process-local caches have explicit TTL/size bounds and invalidation rules.\n\n## Test Matrix\n\nPick the rows that match the work. Do not rely on one happy-path UI test.\n\n| Risk | Test shape |\n| --- | --- |\n| Stale async response | Start request A, switch owner, resolve A, assert it cannot mutate B |\n| Optimistic object erased | Create local optimistic state, apply stale/partial snapshot, assert object remains |\n| Cache masquerades as loaded | Hydrate cache preview, assert full-load gate waits for server authority |\n| Mutation steals navigation | Start mutation, switch selection, resolve mutation, assert focus stays put |\n| Timer/poll stale-fire | Use fake timers or deferred promises; callback must re-check owner before acting |\n| Snapshot merge erases stream | Apply partial snapshot missing known entries, assert entries/read markers remain |\n| Event stream gap | Bootstrap snapshot, emit events during disconnect, reconnect with last id, assert replay or resync |\n| Server decision invisible | Exercise branch and assert named event such as `blocked`, `refused`, `gave_up`, or `error.surfaced` |\n| State classifier wrong | Table-test every status into active/runnable/terminal/recoverable buckets |\n| Fallback duplicates stream | Load fallback rows then stream entries, assert no duplicate or false chronology |\n| Hot path bloats | Assert snapshots carry cursors not bodies; route returns before DB/heavy imports when possible |\n| Timestamp tie | Insert equal timestamps, assert deterministic order by id/seq |\n\nUse headless HTTP/SSE tests for control-plane decisions. Use React/unit tests for manager merging and ownership guards. Use Playwright only for real UI layout, focus, scroll, mobile computed styles, and visible affordances.\n\n## Refuse These Shapes\n\n- one `isLoading` boolean for bootstrap, refresh, reconnect, and recovery;\n- cache or optimistic data treated as authoritative without provenance;\n- async callbacks that mutate state without checking owner tokens;\n- client-side derivation of server decisions;\n- snapshot diffs used as the only way to know what happened;\n- event streams without ids or resume;\n- timestamp-only \"latest\" queries;\n- parallel transcript or persistence surfaces that the frontend must reconcile;\n- unbounded process caches introduced to hide slow storage;\n- tests that prove only the DOM result when the bug is a server decision.\n\n## Review Prompt\n\nAsk this before implementation starts:\n\n> What can arrive late, out of order, partially, twice, or after the user changes context, and what test proves it cannot corrupt visible state?\n/Users/masterman/NLP/omniharness\nAGENTS.md\n M bridge.lock.json\n M docs/codex-history/conversations/2026/08/2026-08-04-claude-fix-auto-scroll-on-new-messages-in-conversation-9951b149-2404-416f-adbe-40d592278c48-part-001.md\n M docs/codex-history/export-events.jsonl\n M docs/codex-history/manifest.json\n M runner.lock.json\n?? docs/codex-history/conversations/2026/08/2026-08-04-claude-debug-session-still-showing-as-working-2251d8a0-2668-43cb-beee-00b466911874-part-001.md\n?? docs/codex-history/conversations/2026/08/2026-08-04-commit-and-push-modified-files-019fcd5e-f28e-7821-b310-2c8af65c9c8f-part-001.md\n-rw-r--r--@ 1 masterman  staff  94937088 Aug  5 10:54 sqlite.db\n-rw-r--r--@ 1 masterman  staff     32768 Aug  5 05:30 sqlite.db-shm\n-rw-r--r--@ 1 masterman  staff  56246272 Aug  5 10:59 sqlite.db-wal\n"

workers_work_timer_start\nAFTER UPDATE OF status, updated_at ON workers\nWHEN lower(substr(OLD.status, 1, instr(OLD.status || ':', ':') - 1)) != 'working'\n  AND lower(substr(NEW.status, 1, instr(NEW.status || ':', ':') - 1)) = 'working'\nBEGIN\n  UPDATE workers\n  SET active_work_started_at = NEW.updated_at,\n      active_work_duration_ms = COALESCE(NEW.active_work_duration_ms, ## Codex progress · 2026-08-05T09:00:28.946Z

The conversation data is intact. The failure is narrower: this morning the app successfully found and resumed the saved Claude session three times, but the worker immediately ended in “Authentication required.” I’m checking why the selected account (`claude-sub-1`) was accepted for recovery even though its credentials were no longer usable.

## Visible reasoning summary · 2026-08-05T09:02:04.370Z

**Diagnosing keychain credential access issue**

<!-- /codex-event -->

<!-- codex-event:{"kind":"progress","timestamp":"2026-08-05T09:02:06.041Z","phase":"commentary"} -->
## Codex progress · 2026-08-05T09:02:06.041Z

I found the concrete mismatch: the selected “local Claude session” account deliberately removes every API/OAuth environment credential, but the runtime only injects the macOS Keychain OAuth token for a different account mode. So recovery can reattach to the saved session, then the first prompt has no usable credential. I’m confirming that against Claude’s own authentication status before changing the account-resolution logic.

<!-- /codex-event -->

## User · 2026-08-05T09:02:35.212Z

Ok so it's Claude's authentication required. That's fine but we need to surface it properly! 

And if OH can run /login in Claude, get the URL, open the local browser (where the interface is running) to do it, then open the redirect callback to therunning Claude Code on the server, that would be amazing

<!-- codex-event:{"kind":"progress","timestamp":"2026-08-05T09:02:46.153Z","phase":"commentary"} -->
## Codex progress · 2026-08-05T09:02:46.153Z

Exactly. The session is recoverable; the app is presenting a Claude credential failure as a generic failed “Ask,” which is the wrong surface.

I’m treating this as two connected fixes: first, turn Claude authentication failures into a clear account action; second, add a sign-in handoff that starts Claude’s login on the runner, opens its authorization URL in the browser running OmniHarness, and returns the authorization result to that same runner process. I’m checking Claude’s current login protocol before wiring it up, because the callback/code exchange has to go back to the machine running Claude, not merely the browser machine.

<!-- /codex-event -->

Use/customize the shadcn control before hand-building a one-off control.\n- When a scrollable or clipped container holds focusable controls, reserve inner space at least as wide as their outer focus ring or draw the ring inset. Padding outside the clipping viewport does not protect descendant focus rings.\n\n## State And Data\n\n- Always centralize React app state in global single-source-of-truth Manager classes.\n- Components, hooks, and listeners subscribe to Manager updates and use Manager methods to update data.\n- Centralized state does not mean broad subscriptions. Components must subscribe to the smallest slice they need, preferably with selector hooks and equality checks, so unrelated UI does not re-render.\n- Never let app shells, page roots, sidebars, timelines, or other broad containers subscribe to an entire high-churn Manager snapshot when they only need stable fields. Draft text, cursors, hover/open state, search text, and other per-keystroke or per-pointer state need narrow component-level subscriptions.\n- If typing in an input, moving a cursor, toggling a hover/open flag, or editing a small draft makes the whole app repaint, the subscription boundary is wrong even if the data source is centralized.\n- Always implement a custom data manager for any app data structure.\n- Never use separate state variables or arrays as the source of truth.\n- Never send data around in transactions. Pass intent, ids, commands, or Manager method calls instead.\n\n## React Control Flow\n\n- Avoid using `useEffect()` to update state. Prefer `useCallback()` handlers or Manager methods for explicit state transitions.\n- Work ahead of time to avoid callback hell, race conditions, and infinite loops.\n- When debugging React and a fix does not work, explicitly consider whether the root cause is a race condition. Race conditions are common in React.\n- Every async callback, mutation result, timer, poll, or stream message that can update visible state must prove it still owns the current context before mutating Managers or navigation.\n- Use `RefObject` instead of `MutableRefObject`.\n\n## Routing And Modules\n\n- Prefer explicit routing over file-based routing when the framework does not strongly default to file-based routes. Framework-native file routing, such as Next.js App Router, is fine.\n- Never use `require()` to import modules. Use `import` instead.\n- Avoid `npm` by default. Use `pnpm` for package installation, scripts, and lockfiles unless the user or existing project explicitly chooses another package manager.\n\n## Next.js And SSR Defaults\n\n- Treat `\"use client\"` as a boundary with a cost. Do not put an entire page, app shell, sidebar, timeline, composer, settings surface, and data boot path behind one giant client component unless there is a deliberate reason.\n- SSR is only useful if the initial HTML contains meaningful above-the-fold UI or data. If `curl` for the route only shows a boot shell, script payloads, or empty placeholders, the route is not meaningfully server-rendered even if SSR is technically enabled.\n- Server-render the first useful screen by fetching route-stable bootstrap data on the server: auth/session state, settings needed to choose the shell, selected route ids, and initial page/snapshot data. Hydrate Managers or query caches from that bootstrap data; do not wait for client `useEffect()` plus client fetches before the first meaningful paint.\n- Use client effects for browser-only concerns: localStorage-only preferences, notifications, SSE/WebSocket subscriptions, focus management, and post-paint refreshes. Do not use effects as the only path for data required to decide what the initial screen is.\n- In Next dev, first request time includes compilation. Distinguish cold compile cost from runtime slowness before optimizing the wrong layer.\n\n## Import And Bundle Hygiene\n\n- Keep imports proportional to the UI being rendered. Do not import an entire complex surface for a tiny control; split shared tiny controls into their own files instead of importing them from heavy feature modules.\n- Lazy-load rarely opened or route-conditional surfaces with dynamic imports: settings dialogs, side panels, file viewers, terminals, worker inspectors, onboarding flows, QR/pairing dialogs, and large markdown/code viewers.\n- Avoid package and barrel imports that drag in whole icon packs, design systems, syntax highlighters, markdown stacks, charting libraries, or component suites for one glyph or one primitive. Inline small SVG paths or use local assets when that is cheaper and stable.\n- Prefer native browser controls for small, ordinary selects and pickers. Do not import a popover/dialog/menu stack for a low-value dropdown unless search, virtualization, or rich item rendering is genuinely needed.\n- Use subpath imports when a package supports them. Avoid root package imports from large UI libraries if a narrower entrypoint exists.\n- After dependency or UI-shell changes, inspect the emitted dev chunks or traces. Verify whether heavy modules moved out of the root chunk rather than assuming dynamic import worked.\n\n## Performance Debugging\n\n- Start with evidence: dev server logs, route timings, emitted chunk sizes, module counts, and initial HTML content.\n- Separate long-lived requests from slow requests. SSE, streaming, and hanging fetches can show huge durations in logs without blocking page paint.\n- Check recent dependency additions first when compile time suddenly explodes. A single icon, markdown, chart, or UI package can add thousands of modules.\n- For Next.js root-load complaints, answer three questions before changing code: what compiled, what HTML was returned, and what client-side requests gated the first useful paint?\n\n## Frontend Configuration\n\n- Never store frontend string literals or UI settings in `.env` files.\n- Put frontend strings and settings in normal source files, inline arrays, or `.json` files so they remain reviewable, testable, and versioned with the UI.\n\n## Internationalization\n\n- For meaningful React UI work, offer language support as an option when the user has not already decided: \"Should we support other languages? If yes, I will wire user-facing copy through i18n resources now instead of hardcoding strings.\"\n- If the user says yes, asks for translation/i18n/language/locale support, or reports that language switching only updates part of the UI, use `implementing-react-i18n`.\n- Use the i18n library's `t(\"key\", params?)` function for every user-facing string. Use `useI18nSnapshot()` only to subscribe React components so language changes repaint.\n- Never name variables with locale codes or locale-like names such as `it`, `en`, `enUS`, `locIt`, or `locEnUs`.\n\n## Settings UI\n\n- For settings dialogs, preferences panels, configuration modals, option tabs, or UI settings surfaces, use `designing-settings-dialogs`.\n---\nname: test-driven-development\ndescription: Use when implementing any feature or bugfix, before writing implementation code\n---\n\n# Test-Driven Development (TDD)\n\n## Overview\n\nWrite the test first. Watch it fail. Write minimal code to pass.\n\n**Core principle:** If you didn't watch the test fail, you don't know if it tests the right thing.\n\n**Violating the letter of the rules is violating the spirit of the rules.**\n\n## When to Use\n\n**Always:**\n- New features\n- Bug fixes\n- Refactoring\n- Behavior changes\n\n**Exceptions (ask your human partner):**\n- Throwaway prototypes\n- Generated code\n- Configuration files\n\n**Do directly with targeted verification:**\n- tiny color, spacing, copy, or label changes,\n- one-line prompt wording changes,\n- docs-only wording changes,\n- mechanical edits where the expected result is obvious from the diff.\n\nDo not perform a full red-green cycle for a tiny mechanical edit unless it changes behavior or there is a real regression risk. For example, changing a button from gray to blue usually needs the edit plus a targeted visual or code check, not a new failing test.\n\nThinking \"skip TDD just this once\"? Stop. That's rationalization.\n\n## UI-Specific Note\n\nWhen the task is UI work, choose the design system and starting block before writing tests around the intended behavior and rendering. In Ultrapowers, that means `shadcn/ui` plus the closest available ShadCN Block for each new screen, form, or major surface unless the user says otherwise. Inspect https://ui.shadcn.com/blocks first, install or copy the selected block, and then adapt it. Before adding any individual control, check whether `shadcn/ui` already provides a suitable control and use/customize that control first.\n\nFor important app workflows, also consider whether `agentic-user-journey-testing` should be proposed. Do not run that higher-cost black-box test mode without explicit user approval.\n\n## The Iron Law\n\n```\nNO PRODUCTION CODE WITHOUT A FAILING TEST FIRST\n```\n\nWrite code before the test? Delete it. Start over.\n\n**No exceptions:**\n- Don't keep it as \"reference\"\n- Don't \"adapt\" it while writing tests\n- Don't look at it\n- Delete means delete\n\nImplement fresh from tests. Period.\n\n## Red-Green-Refactor\n\n```dot\ndigraph tdd_cycle {\n    rankdir=LR;\n    red [label=\"RED\\nWrite failing test\", shape=box, style=filled, fillcolor=\"#ffcccc\"];\n    verify_red [label=\"Verify fails\\ncorrectly\", shape=diamond];\n    green [label=\"GREEN\\nMinimal code\", shape=box, style=filled, fillcolor=\"#ccffcc\"];\n    verify_green [label=\"Verify passes\\nAll green\", shape=diamond];\n    refactor [label=\"REFACTOR\\nClean up\", shape=box, style=filled, fillcolor=\"#ccccff\"];\n    next [label=\"Next\", shape=ellipse];\n\n    red -> verify_red;\n    verify_red -> green [label=\"yes\"];\n    verify_red -> red [label=\"wrong\\nfailure\"];\n    green -> verify_green;\n    verify_green -> refactor [label=\"yes\"];\n    verify_green -> green [label=\"no\"];\n    refactor -> verify_green [label=\"stay\\ngreen\"];\n    verify_green -> next;\n    next -> red;\n}\n```\n\n### RED - Write Failing Test\n\nWrite one minimal test showing what should happen.\n\n<Good>\n```typescript\ntest('retries failed operations 3 times', async () => {\n  let attempts = 0;\n  const operation = () => {\n    attempts++;\n    if (attempts < 3) throw new Error('fail');\n    return 'success';\n  };\n\n  const result = await retryOperation(operation);\n\n  expect(result).toBe('success');\n  expect(attempts).toBe(3);\n});\n```\nClear name, tests real behavior, one thing\n</Good>\n\n<Bad>\n```typescript\ntest('retry works', async () => {\n  const mock = jest.fn()\n    .mockRejectedValueOnce(new Error())\n    .mockRejectedValueOnce(new Error())\n    .mockResolvedValueOnce('success');\n  await retryOperation(mock);\n  expect(mock).toHaveBeenCalledTimes(3);\n});\n```\nVague name, tests mock not code\n</Bad>\n\n**Requirements:**\n- One behavior\n- Clear name\n- Real code (no mocks unless unavoidable)\n- Real product behavior is the target. Mocked behavior can never be the final deliverable or proof that the feature works.\n\n### Verify RED - Watch It Fail\n\n**MANDATORY. Never skip.**\n\n```bash\nnpm test path/to/test.test.ts\n```\n\nConfirm:\n- Test fails (not errors)\n- Failure message is expected\n- Fails because feature missing (not typos)\n\n**Test passes?** You're testing existing behavior. Fix test.\n\n**Test errors?** Fix error, re-run until it fails correctly.\n\n### GREEN - Minimal Code\n\nWrite simplest code to pass the test.\n\n<Good>\n```typescript\nasync function retryOperation<T>(fn: () => Promise<T>): Promise<T> {\n  for (let i = 0; i < 3; i++) {\n    try {\n      return await fn();\n    } catch (e) {\n      if (i === 2) throw e;\n    }\n  }\n  throw new Error('unreachable');\n}\n```\nJust enough to pass\n</Good>\n\n<Bad>\n```typescript\nasync function retryOperation<T>(\n  fn: () => Promise<T>,\n  options?: {\n    maxRetries?: number;\n    backoff?: 'linear' | 'exponential';\n    onRetry?: (attempt: number) => void;\n  }\n): Promise<T> {\n  // YAGNI\n}\n```\nOver-engineered\n</Bad>\n\nDon't add features, refactor other code, or \"improve\" beyond the test.\n\n### Verify GREEN - Watch It Pass\n\n**MANDATORY.**\n\n```bash\nnpm test path/to/test.test.ts\n```\n\nConfirm:\n- Test passes\n- Other tests still pass\n- Output pristine (no errors, warnings)\n\n**Test fails?** Fix code, not test.\n\n**Other tests fail?** Fix now.\n\n### REFACTOR - Clean Up\n\nAfter green only:\n- Remove duplication\n- Improve names\n- Extract helpers\n\nKeep tests green. Don't add behavior.\n\n### Repeat\n\nNext failing test for next feature.\n\n## Good Tests\n\n| Quality | Good | Bad |\n|---------|------|-----|\n| **Minimal** | One thing. \"and\" in name? Split it. | `test('validates email and domain and whitespace')` |\n| **Clear** | Name describes behavior | `test('test1')` |\n| **Shows intent** | Demonstrates desired API | Obscures what code should do |\n\n## Why Order Matters\n\n**\"I'll write tests after to verify it works\"**\n\nTests written after code pass immediately. Passing immediately proves nothing:\n- Might test wrong thing\n- Might test implementation, not behavior\n- Might miss edge cases you forgot\n- You never saw it catch the bug\n\nTest-first forces you to see the test fail, proving it actually tests something.\n\n**\"I already manually tested all the edge cases\"**\n\nManual testing is ad-hoc. You think you tested everything but:\n- No record of what you tested\n- Can't re-run when code changes\n- Easy to forget cases under pressure\n- \"It worked when I tried it\" ≠ comprehensive\n\nAutomated tests are systematic. They run the same way every time.\n\n**\"Deleting X hours of work is wasteful\"**\n\nSunk cost fallacy. The time is already gone. Your choice now:\n- Delete and rewrite with TDD (X more hours, high confidence)\n- Keep it and add tests after (30 min, low confidence, likely bugs)\n\nThe \"waste\" is keeping code you can't trust. Working code without real tests is technical debt.\n\n**\"TDD is dogmatic, being pragmatic means adapting\"**\n\nTDD IS pragmatic:\n- Finds bugs before commit (faster than debugging after)\n- Prevents regressions (tests catch breaks immediately)\n- Documents behavior (tests show how to use code)\n- Enables refactoring (change freely, tests catch breaks)\n\n\"Pragmatic\" shortcuts = debugging in production = slower.\n\n**\"Tests after achieve the same goals - it's spirit not ritual\"**\n\nNo. Tests-after answer \"What does this do?\" Tests-first answer \"What should this do?\"\n\nTests-after are biased by your implementation. You test what you built, not what's required. You verify remembered edge cases, not discovered ones.\n\nTests-first force edge case discovery before implementing. Tests-after verify you remembered everything (you didn't).\n\n30 minutes of tests after ≠ TDD. You get coverage, lose proof tests work.\n\n## Common Rationalizations\n\n| Excuse | Reality |\n|--------|---------|\n| \"Too simple to test\" | Simple code breaks. Test takes 30 seconds. |\n| \"I'll test after\" | Tests passing immediately prove nothing. |\n| \"Tests after achieve same goals\" | Tests-after = \"what does this do?\" Tests-first = \"what should this do?\" |\n| \"Already manually tested\" | Ad-hoc ≠ systematic. No record, can't re-run. |\n| \"Deleting X hours is wasteful\" | Sunk cost fallacy. Keeping unverified code is technical debt. |\n| \"Keep as reference, write tests first\" | You'll adapt it. That's testing after. Delete means delete. |\n| \"Need to explore first\" | Fine. Throw away exploration, start with TDD. |\n| \"Test hard = design unclear\" | Listen to test. Hard to test = hard to use. |\n| \"TDD will slow me down\" | TDD faster than debugging. Pragmatic = test-first. |\n| \"Manual test faster\" | Manual doesn't prove edge cases. You'll re-test every change. |\n| \"Existing code has no tests\" | You're improving it. Add tests for existing code. |\n\n## Red Flags - STOP and Start Over\n\n- Code before test\n- Test after implementation\n- Test passes immediately\n- Can't explain why test failed\n- Tests added \"later\"\n- Rationalizing \"just this once\"\n- \"I already manually tested it\"\n- \"Tests after achieve the same purpose\"\n- \"It's about spirit not ritual\"\n- \"Keep as reference\" or \"adapt existing code\"\n- \"Already spent X hours, deleting is wasteful\"\n- \"TDD is dogmatic, I'm being pragmatic\"\n- \"This is different because...\"\n\n**All of these mean: Delete code. Start over with TDD.**\n\n## Example: Bug Fix\n\n**Bug:** Empty email accepted\n\n**RED**\n```typescript\ntest('rejects empty email', async () => {\n  const result = await submitForm({ email: '' });\n  expect(result.error).toBe('Email required');\n});\n```\n\n**Verify RED**\n```bash\n$ npm test\nFAIL: expected 'Email required', got undefined\n"
  }
]

SDK\n\n[![npm](https://img.shields.io/npm/v/%40agentclientprotocol%2Fclaude-agent-acp)](https://www.npmjs.com/package/@agentclientprotocol/claude-agent-acp)\n\nUse [Claude Agent <!-- codex-event:{"kind":"progress","timestamp":"2026-08-05T09:04:01.130Z","phase":"commentary"} -->
## Codex progress · 2026-08-05T09:04:01.130Z

Claude’s ACP adapter already advertises a terminal-based sign-in method, but OmniHarness currently does not declare that it can handle terminal authentication, so the method is hidden. For a remote runner, Claude intentionally uses a copy-back login flow rather than a localhost callback, because a browser redirect to `localhost` would land on the browser machine instead of the runner.

I’m probing that exact remote flow now. This will determine whether OmniHarness can complete it automatically or whether the secure first version needs one explicit “paste authorization code” step.

<!-- /codex-event -->

    "text": "Authentication - Claude Platform Docs (https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/manage-claude/authentication)\nciteturn0search0 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: last week; # Authentication ... Choose API keys to get started quickly, and move to Workload Identity Federation when your workload already has a platform-issued identity you can federate. ... The workload exchanges its IdP-issued JWT at `POST /v1/oauth/token` for a short-lived Claude API access token, and the SDK refreshes that token automatically before it expires.\n\n# Authentication\n\nAuthenticate to the Claude API with API keys or Workload Identity Federation.\n\nCopy page\n\n\n\nThe Claude API supports two ways to authenticate requests:\n\nMethod  | Credential  | Best for\n--- | --- | ---\nAPI key  | Static `sk-ant-api...` secret in the `x-api-key` header  | Local development, prototyping, scripts, and single-tenant servers where you control secret storage\nWorkload Identity Federation  | Short-lived bearer token exchanged from your identity provider's identity token  | Production workloads on cloud platforms (AWS, Google Cloud, Azure), CI/CD pipelines, and Kubernetes, where you want to eliminate static secrets\n\nBoth methods grant the same access to Claude API endpoints. Choose API keys to get started quickly, and move to Workload Identity Federation when your workload already has a platform-issued identity you can federate.\n\n\nAPI keys\n\nAPI keys are static secrets that you generate in the Claude Console and pass on every request.\n\n  * Create a key: Go to Settings → API keys in the Claude Console. You choose an expiration as part of creation. Use workspaces to scope keys by project or environment.\n  * Send the key: Set the `x-api-key` header on direct HTTP requests, or set the `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` environment variable and the client SDKs pick it up automatically.\n    \n    `POST /v1/messages\n    x-api-key: [REDACTED]    anthropic-version: 2023-06-01\n    content-type: application/json`\n\n\n\nStore API keys in a secrets manager, rotate them periodically, and revoke any key you suspect has leaked. You can also set an expiration when you create a key to limit how long a leaked credential stays usable.\n\n[Button: cURL][Button: Python][Button: TypeScript][Button: Go][Button: Java][Button: C#][Button: PHP][Button: Ruby][Button: CLI]\n\n\n    \n    `client = Anthropic(api_key=\"my-anthropic-api-key\")\n    # or, with ANTHROPIC_API_KEY set in the environment:\n    client = Anthropic()`\n\nKey expiration\n\nWhen you create an API key from the API keys page in the Claude Console, you choose an expiration: a preset (3 hours, 1 day, 7 days, or 30 days), a custom duration, or Never for keys you store in a secrets manager and rotate yourself. If your organization has a maximum expiration policy, the Console limits presets and custom durations to the policy maximum, and Never is unavailable. Existing keys keep their current behavior; expiration is set at creation time and cannot be changed afterward. The same expiration choice applies when you create an Admin API key in the Claude Console.\n\nAnthropic emails the key's creator as the expiration approaches: 7 days before expiration for keys created with a lifetime of at least 14 days, and 1 day before for keys with a lifetime of at least 7 days. Keys with shorter lifetimes expire without a warning email.\n\nAfter a key expires, requests made with it return a `401 authentication_error`. Create a new key to restore access; expired keys cannot be reactivated.\n\nThe Console API keys table shows each key's expiration, and the Admin API reports each key's `expires_at` timestamp on the List API Keys and Retrieve API Key endpoints, so you can audit and rotate keys before they expire. The field is `null` for keys without an expiration.\n\nExpiration limits the lifetime of a leaked credential, but it is not a substitute for secret hygiene. Regardless of expiration, store keys in a secrets manager and revoke any key you suspect has leaked.\n\n\nWorkload Identity Federation\n\nWorkload Identity Federation (WIF) lets a workload authenticate with a short-lived identity token issued by an identity provider (IdP) you already trust, such as AWS IAM, Google Cloud, or any standards-compliant OIDC issuer (such as GitHub Actions, Kubernetes service accounts, SPIFFE, Microsoft Entra ID, or Okta). The workload exchanges its IdP-issued JWT at `POST /v1/oauth/token` for a short-lived Claude API access token, and the SDK refreshes that token automatically before it expires. There is no `sk-ant-api...` string to mint, distribute, or rotate.\n\nFederation removes long-lived Claude API keys from your environment, which shrinks the blast radius of a leaked credential and lets you manage access with the same IdP controls you already use for cloud resources. It does not, on its own, guarantee end-to-end security: the trust chain is only as strong as your identity provider's configuration, and a long-lived secret one hop upstream (for example, a static cloud credential that can mint IdP tokens) can still undermine it. Pair federation with your provider's controls, such as IP allowlists, MFA, and audit logging.\n\nTo configure federation, you create three resources in the Claude Console (a service account, a federation issuer, and a federation rule) and then point your SDK at the rule. See Workload Identity Federation for the full setup walkthrough.\n\n\nNext steps\n\nSet up Workload Identity Federation Configure issuers, rules, and service accounts, then exchange tokensIdentity provider guides Step-by-step guides for AWS, Google Cloud, Azure, GitHub Actions, Kubernetes, SPIFFE, and OktaWIF reference Environment variables, validation rules, profile configuration, and error referenceClient SDKs Python, TypeScript, C#, Go, Java, PHP, Ruby, and the CLI--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nCLI authentication options - Claude Platform Docs (https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/cli-sdks-libraries/cli/authentication)\nciteturn0search1 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: last week; On a remote host or in any environment without a local browser, pass `--no-browser` to print the authorize URL and paste the returned code back into the terminal. ... `ant auth status` prints the credential source the CLI selected (API key environment variable, OAuth login, federation, or profile), the active profile, the workspace the active token is bound to, and the configuration directory paths. ... Using the CLI Command structure, output formats, GJSON transforms, and request bodiesCLI scripting and automation Version-control API resources, scripting patterns, and use from Claude Code Workload Identity Federation Non-interactive authentication for CI, servers, and containers\n\n# CLI authentication options\n\nAuthenticate the ant CLI with interactive login, API keys, named profiles, and Workload Identity Federation.\n\nCopy page\n\n\n\nThe `ant` CLI supports several credential sources. The Quickstart covers the one-command happy path (`ant auth login`). This page covers every option in full.\n\n## \n\nInteractive login\n\n`ant auth login` lets you call the API without creating or managing an API key. It opens a browser-based OAuth flow against the Claude Console and stores the resulting credentials under `$ANTHROPIC_CONFIG_DIR` (see Configuration directory for the OS-specific default). On a remote host or in any environment without a local browser, pass `--no-browser` to print the authorize URL and paste the returned code back into the terminal.\n\nCLI\n\n\n    \n    `ant auth login\n    \n    # On a remote host without a browser:\n    ant auth login --no-browser\n    \n    # Bind to a specific workspace and skip the browser picker:\n    ant auth login --workspace-id wrkspc_01...\n    \n    # If the named profile you pass with --profile doesn't exist,\n    # a new named profile will be created with that name.\n    ant auth login --profile <profile-nam e>`\n\nDuring the browser flow, you select an organization and then a workspace. The issued token is scoped to that workspace, so the CLI can only see resources that belong to it. Pass `--workspace-id` to bind directly and skip the picker. To work in more than one workspace, see Switch between workspaces.\n\nInteractive login is intended for local development and scripting on your own machine. For non-interactive workloads such as CI, servers, and containers, use Workload Identity Federation instead.\n\nLogin writes credentials to `credentials/<profile>.json`. The first login for a profile also creates `configs/<profile>.json` and sets it as the active profile. To remove stored credentials, run `ant auth logout`, or `ant auth logout --all` to clear every profile.\n\n## \n\nAdmin access\n\nBy default, `ant auth login` requests a workspace-scoped token. To manage the resources documented on the Admin API page, request the `org:admin` scope under a dedicated profile:\n\nCLI\n\n\n    \n    `ant auth login --profile admin --scope \"org:admin\"\n    \n    # Print a bearer token for Authorization headers:\n    ant auth print-credentials --profile admin --access-token`\n\nThe `org:admin` scope is granted only to organization members with the admin, owner, or primary owner role. The issued token has organization-wide access, and any workspace binding on the profile does not constrain it. Keep the admin profile separate from your day-to-day profile so routine commands never run with elevated access.\n\n## \n\nAPI key\n\nThe CLI also reads your API key from the `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` environment variable. Get a key from the Claude Console.\n\nzsh\n\nzsh\n\nbash\n\nbash\n\nWindows\n\nWindows\n    \n    `echo 'export ANTHROPIC_API_KEY=sk-ant-api03-...' >> ~/.zshrc\n    source ~/.zshrc`\n\n\n\nTo override the key for a single invocation, pass `--api-key`. To point at a different API host, set `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` or pass `--base-url`.\n\n## \n\nCheck authentication status\n\n`ant auth status` prints the credential source the CLI selected (API key environment variable, OAuth login, federation, or profile), the active profile, the workspace the active token is bound to, and the configuration directory paths. Use it to diagnose why a workload picked the wrong credential or workspace.\n\nCLI\n\n\n    \n    `ant auth status`\n    \n    `Active profile:  default\n    Config dir:      ~/.config/anthropic\n    Profile config:  ~/.config/anthropic/configs/default.json\n    Credentials:     ~/.config/anthropic/credentials/default.json\n    \n    Credentials\n      (active) * Profile (user_oauth) [via active_config]       sk-ant-oat01-EXA...\n    ...\n    \n    Workspace\n      (active) * Workspace                                      wrkspc_01... (Engineering)`\n\n\n\nRead the `(active)` rows to see which credential source and workspace won. The command reports status rather than performing a health check, so don't script against the exit status. For the full ordering of credential sources, see Credential precedence.\n\n## \n\nSwitch between workspaces\n\nAn interactive-login token is bound to a single workspace. To use the CLI against more than one workspace, log in to each under its own named profile, then switch between them:\n\nCLI\n\n\n    \n    `# 1. Create the profile (interactive; pick the other workspace in the\n    #    browser, or pass --workspace-id to skip the picker):\n    # ant auth login --profile other-ws\n    \n    # 2. Make it the default for subsequent commands:\n    ant profile activate other-ws\n    \n    # 3. Or select it for a single command without changing the default:\n    ant --profile other-ws models list\n    ANTHROPIC_PROFILE=other-ws ant models list`\n\nRun `ant auth status` to confirm which profile and workspace are active.\n\n\n\nProfiles are only consulted when no API key is set. If `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` is present in your environment, it overrides every profile and these commands all use whatever workspace that key is scoped to. Unset it before switching profiles.\n\n## \n\nManage profiles\n\nThe `ant profile` subcommands inspect and edit profile state directly:\n\nCLI\n\n\n    \n    `ant profile list\n    ant profile get --profile other-ws\n    ant profile set workspace_id wrkspc_01... --profile other-ws`\n\nThe writable keys for `ant profile set` are `workspace_id`, `base_url`, `organization_id`, `scope`, `client_id`, and `console_url`. Setting `workspace_id` records the target workspace in the profile config but does not rebind credentials that were already issued; run `ant auth login` again under that profile to mint a token for the new workspace.\n\nFor the profile file schema and the federation block, see Profile configuration file. For Workload Identity Federation, see the Authentication overview and the WIF reference.\n\n## \n\nNext steps\n\nUsing the CLI Command structure, output formats, GJSON transforms, and request bodiesCLI scripting and automation Version-control API resources, scripting patterns, and use from Claude CodeWorkload Identity Federation Non-interactive authentication for CI, servers, and containers\n\nWas this page helpful?--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nCLI scripting and automation - Claude Platform Docs (https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/cli-sdks-libraries/cli/scripting)\nciteturn0search2 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: last week; Version-control API resources as YAML, chain ant CLI commands in scripts, operate on resources from Claude Code, and authenticate curl calls with CLI credentials. ... Run `ant auth status` to confirm which organization and workspace you are logged in to; it warns when an environment variable is overriding your login.\n\nCLI, SDKs, and librariesScripting and automation\n\n# CLI scripting and automation\n\nVersion-control API resources as YAML, chain ant CLI commands in scripts, operate on resources from Claude Code, and authenticate curl calls with CLI credentials.\n\nCopy page\n\n\n\nThis page covers task-oriented workflows built on the `ant` CLI. For the underlying flags and output options, see Using the CLI.\n\n## \n\nVersion-controlling API resources\n\nYou can use the CLI to version control API resources such as skills, agents, environments, or deployments as YAML files in your repository and keep them in sync with the Claude API.\n\n\n\nFor more information on these resources, see Managed Agents.\n\n  1. 1\n\nDefine your agent\n\nWrite the agent definition to `summarizer.agent.yaml`:\n\nsummarizer.agent.yaml\n\n\n    \n    `name: Summarizer\n    model: claude-opus-5\n    system: |\n      You are a helpful assistant that writes concise summaries.\n    tools:\n      - type: agent_toolset_20260401`\n\n  2. 2\n\nCreate the agent\nbeta=true\": 404 Not Found\n    Agent not found.`\n\n## \n\nUse the CLI from Claude Code\n\nClaude Code can use the `ant` CLI out of the box. With the CLI installed and authenticated, you can ask Claude Code to operate on your API resources directly. For example:\n\n  * \"List my recent agent sessions and summarize which ones errored.\"\n  * \"Upload every PDF in `./reports` to the Files API and print the resulting IDs.\"\n  * \"Pull the events for session `session_01...` and tell me where the agent got stuck.\"\n\nClaude Code shells out to `ant`, parses the structured output, and reasons over the results (no custom integration code required).\n\n## \n\nAuthenticate curl requests with CLI credentials\n\nScripts that call the API with `curl` or another HTTP client can use the credentials stored by `ant auth login` instead of a static API key. The OAuth access token goes in the `Authorization` header as a bearer token; the `x-api-key` header is only for static API keys.\n\n`ant auth print-credentials --access-token` prints the active profile's access token, refreshing it first if it is expired or near expiry:\n\ncURL\n\n\n    \n    `curl https://api.anthropic.com/v1/messages \\\n      -H \"Authorization: Bearer $(ant auth print-credentials --access-token)\" \\\n      -H \"anthropic-version: 2023-06-01\" \\\n      -H \"content-type: application/json\" \\\n      -d '{\n        \"model\": \"claude-opus-5\",\n        \"max_tokens\": 256,\n        \"messages\": [{\"role\": \"user\", \"content\": \"hi\"}]\n      }'`\n\n\n\nKeep `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` and `ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN` unset when working from a CLI login. Either variable takes precedence over the login for `ant` commands (see Credential precedence) and can silently route them to a different organization or workspace.\n\nRun `ant auth status` to confirm which organization and workspace you are logged in to; it warns when an environment variable is overriding your login.\n\nWas this page helpful?--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nTrigger a routine through the API - Claude Platform Docs (https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/api/claude-code/routines-fire)\nciteturn0search3 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: last week; Authentication  | `Authorization: Bearer` with a per-routine token (`sk-ant-oat01-...`) created at claude.ai/code/routines  | `x-api-key` with a Claude API key from Claude Console ... Billing  | Claude Code subscription usage on claude.ai  | Claude Platform usage ... HTTP status  | Error type  | Cause\n\n# Trigger a routine through the API\n\nStart a Claude Code routine session on demand by sending an authenticated POST request.\n\nCopy page\n\n\n\n\n\nThis is an experimental API. Request and response shapes, rate limits, and token semantics might change. Breaking changes ship behind new dated beta header versions, and the two previous header versions continue to work so that callers have time to migrate.\n\nClaude Code is Anthropic's agentic coding tool. Claude Code on the web runs Claude Code sessions on Anthropic-managed cloud infrastructure at claude.ai/code, and a routine is a saved configuration there: a prompt, one or more repositories, and connectors, packaged so it can run unattended on a schedule, in response to GitHub events, or when called over HTTP.\n\nThis endpoint is the HTTP entry point. POSTing to it starts a new run of an existing routine and returns the resulting session ID and URL. Typical callers are alerting systems, CI pipelines, and internal tools that need to start a Claude Code session programmatically.\n\nCalling this endpoint requires a claude.ai account on a Pro, Max, Team, or Enterprise plan with Claude Code on the web enabled. Authenticate with a per-routine bearer token created in the Claude Code web UI rather than a Claude API key.\n\n## \n\nDifferences from the Claude Platform\n\nThe routine fire endpoint belongs to the Claude Code product surface, which differs from the Claude Platform APIs and SDKs in a few ways:\n\nAspect  | This endpoint  | Claude Platform APIs\n--- | --- | ---\nAuthentication  | `Authorization: Bearer` with a per-routine token (`sk-ant-oat01-...`) created at claude.ai/code/routines  | `x-api-key` with a Claude API key from Claude Console\nToken scope  | One routine only; no read access  | Workspace-level\nSDK support  | None  | Available in all client SDKs\nBilling  | Claude Code subscription usage on claude.ai  | Claude Platform usage\nPath namespace  | `/v1/claude_code/...`  | `/v1/...`\nStability  | Experimental; requires `anthropic-beta: experimental-cc-routine-2026-04-01`  | Stable or standard beta\n\n## \n\nBefore you begin\n\nTo call this endpoint, you need:\n\n  1. A routine created at claude.ai/code/routines.\n  2. A bearer token generated for that routine: open the routine for editing, click Add another trigger under Select a trigger, choose API, then click Generate token in the modal window. The token is shown once and cannot be retrieved later.\n\nSee Add an API trigger in the Claude Code documentation for the full setup walkthrough.\n\n## \n\nTrigger a routine\n    \n    `POST https://api.anthropic.com/v1/claude_code/routines/{routine_id}/fire`\n\n\n\nEvery request must include the `anthropic-beta: experimental-cc-routine-2026-04-01` header. Requests without it return `400 invalid_request_error`.\n\nThe Claude Code web UI provides the full URL alongside the token when you add an API trigger, so most integrations store both as secrets and call the endpoint directly. The following examples show a shell call and a GitHub Actions step that triggers the routine on CI failure.\n\ncURL\n\n\n    \n    `curl -X POST https://api.anthropic.com/v1/claude_code/routines/$ROUTINE_ID/fire \\\n      -H \"Authorization: Bearer $ROUTINE_TOKEN\" \\\n      -H \"anthropic-version: 2023-06-01\" \\\n      -H \"anthropic-beta: experimental-cc-routine-2026-04-01\" \\\n      -H \"Content-Type: application/json\" \\\n      -d '{\"text\": \"Sentry alert SEN-4521 fired in prod. Stack trace attached.\"}'`\n\nGitHub Actions\n\n\n    \n    `- if: failure()\n      env:\n        ROUTINE_FIRE_URL: ${{ secrets.ROUTINE_FIRE_URL }}\n        ROUTINE_FIRE_TOKEN: ${{ secrets.ROUTINE_FIRE_TOKEN }}\n      run: |\n        curl -X POST \"$ROUTINE_FIRE_URL\" \\\n          -H \"Authorization: Bearer $ROUTINE_FIRE_TOKEN\" \\\n          -H \"anthropic-version: 2023-06-01\" \\\n          -H \"anthropic-beta: experimental-cc-routine-2026-04-01\" \\\n          -H \"Content-Type: application/json\" \\\n          -d \"{\\\"text\\\": \\\"CI failed: $GITHUB_WORKFLOW run $GITHUB_RUN_ID on $GITHUB_REF\\\"}\"`\n\nThe request returns once the session is created. It does not stream session output or wait for the session to complete.\n\n### \n\nHeaders\n\nName  | Required  | Description\n--- | --- | ---\n`Authorization`  | Yes  | `Bearer <token>`. The per-routine token created in the Claude Code web UI, prefixed `sk-ant-oat01-`.\n`anthropic-beta`  | Yes  | Must include `experimental-cc-routine-2026-04-01`.\n`anthropic-version`  | Yes  | The API version, for example `2023-06-01`.\n`Content-Type`  | When body is present  | `application/json`.\n\n### \n\nPath parameters\n\nName  | Type  | Description\n--- | --- | ---\n`claude_code_session_id`  | string  | The ID of the Claude Code session created for this run.\n`claude_code_session_url`  | string  | A link to the session on claude.ai. Open it in a browser to watch the run, review changes, or continue the conversation.\n\n### \n\nErrors\n\nErrors use the standard Anthropic error envelope:\n    \n    `{\n      \"type\": \"error\",\n      \"error\": {\n        \"type\": \"not_found_error\",\n        \"message\": \"<string>\"\n      }\n    }`\n\n\n\nHTTP status  | Error type  | Cause\n--- | --- | ---\n400  | `invalid_request_error`  | Missing or invalid `anthropic-beta` header, `text` exceeds 65,536 characters, or the routine is paused (see Edit and control routines).\n401  | `authentication_error`  | No bearer token in the `Authorization` header, or the token does not match this routine.\n403  | `permission_error`  | The account or organization does not have access to this endpoint.\n404  | `not_found_error`  | The routine does not exist.\n429  | `rate_limit_error`  | The account's routine run limit or usage limit has been reached. The response includes a `Retry-After` header indicating when the window resets.\n500  | `api_error`  | An unexpected server error. Retry with exponential backoff; if the error persists, contact support with the request ID.\n503  | `overloaded_error`  | The service is temporarily overloaded. Retry after a short delay. The Claude Platform returns 529 for this error type; this endpoint returns 503.\n\n## \n\nAuthentication\n\nThe bearer token is scoped to a single routine. A compromised token can only trigger that routine; it grants no read access, no access to other routines, and no access to account data.\n\nGenerate and revoke tokens from the routine's API trigger settings at claude.ai/code/routines. There is no public API for token management. Generating a new token revokes the previous one.\n\n## \n\nIdempotency\n\nEach successful request creates a new session. There is no idempotency key. If a webhook caller retries, the endpoint creates multiple sessions.\n\n## \n\nRate limits\n\nRoutine runs count against a per-account daily allowance that varies by plan, and the resulting sessions draw down the same Claude Code subscription usage as interactive sessions. When either limit is reached, the endpoint returns `429 rate_limit_error` with a `Retry-After` header. Organizations with extra usage enabled continue past the included allowance on metered overage.\n\nView your remaining daily runs at claude.ai/code/routines. For how routine usage interacts with subscription limits and extra usage billing, see Usage and limits in the Claude Code documentation.\n\n## \n\nSDK support\n\nThis endpoint is not in the Anthropic SDKs. Its token model differs from API key authentication, and typical callers such as CI jobs and alerting webhooks send the request directly.\n\n## \n\nSee also\n\n  * Automate work with routines in the Claude Code documentation--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nSet up Claude Code - Anthropic (https://docs.anthropic.com/en/docs/claude-code/getting-started)\nciteturn0search4 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: last year;   * Network: Internet connection required for authentication and AI processing ... Claude Platform on AWS  | Yes  | Claude Platform on AWS generates Access Transparency events within the Compliance API (not AWS CloudTrail)\nThe design rests on the following principles:\n\n  * Human access happens only under a published reason code.\n  * Human views of your covered content are recorded. Anthropic's internal tooling that can reach your covered content is instrumented to emit an event on each view.\n  * Events represent human access, not automated processing. Anthropic's automated safety systems process your content in a secured pipeline with no interactive human access; that processing does not generate `anthropic_access` events. The one event automated processing can initiate is a `cmek_preserve` preservation record (see CMEK content preservation).\n  * Events arrive on your existing feed. Activities are accessible through your Compliance API Activity Feed.
