````text
    "text": "# PM Pass\n\nUse this reference before the completeness pass for app, UI, and product-surface work. The goal is to think like a strong product manager: understand what the product wants to become, discover the supporting jobs around the core job, and preserve that larger vision even when delivery is staged.\n\nIn Ultrapowers, this is a required default move for app, UI, and product-surface planning. If that kind of work is happening, the PM pass should be visible in the design output rather than silently skipped.\n\n## Core Assumptions\n\n- The first user is usually the human builder unless the prompt says otherwise.\n- The core job is usually already implied by the request.\n- The missing work is often in the supporting jobs, state model, trust model, and operational reality.\n- Agents also tend to under-specify persistence. Counteract that tendency by making storage and lifecycle decisions explicit for settings and user-managed state.\n- Agents also tend to over-explain settings UI. Counteract that tendency by treating settings dialogs as control surfaces, not narrative pages.\n- Agents tend to underbuild unless forced to think beyond the literal prompt. Counteract that tendency here.\n\n## PM Pass Outputs\n\nProduce or infer these sections when relevant:\n\n- First User\n- Core Job\n- Supporting Jobs\n- User Segmentation And Role Clarity\n- State Model\n- Persistence Model\n- Operational Readiness\n- Instrumentation And Observability\n- Onboarding And Discoverability\n- Risk And Trust Surfaces\n- North Star Product\n- Current Milestone\n- Future Product Direction\n\n## What Each Section Means\n\n### First User\n\nWho is the first person this is really being built for right now?\n\nDefault: the human builder.\n\n### Core Job\n\nWhat is the one main job the first user wants to accomplish?\n\nDo not waste time rediscovering what is already obvious from the prompt.\n\n### Supporting Jobs\n\nWhat other jobs must the product support so the core job is actually workable in practice?\n\nThese often include:\n\n- returning to prior work,\n- recovering from failure,\n- understanding current status,\n- revising earlier state,\n- monitoring progress,\n- discovering what to do next.\n\n### User Segmentation And Role Clarity\n\nOnly go deep here when the product actually has multiple roles. Do not invent fake role complexity for single-user tools.\n\n### State Model\n\nDefine the meaningful product states, not just visual states.\n\nExamples:\n\n- draft,\n- queued,\n- running,\n- waiting-for-user,\n- failed,\n- completed,\n- archived,\n- forked.\n\n### Persistence Model\n\nDefine where meaningful settings, preferences, drafts, and user-managed state live and how they behave over time.\n\nAsk explicitly:\n\n- does this setting persist at all,\n- is it device-local, browser-local, file-based, workspace-local, or account-level,\n- what is the source of truth,\n- how is it serialized and versioned,\n- when is it loaded and saved,\n- how are conflicts, resets, migrations, and deletions handled,\n- what should survive refresh, restart, sign-out, reinstall, or device change.\n\n### Operational Readiness\n\nAsk what must work when the real world is messy:\n\n- transient failures,\n- missing config,\n- degraded providers,\n- long-running work,\n- partial completion,\n- retries and re-entry.\n\n### Instrumentation And Observability\n\nAsk what signals should exist so both the user and the builder can understand what is happening.\n\nThis includes:\n\n- user-visible status,\n- key logs or events,\n- enough observability to debug failures and stuck states.\n- durable event history for backend actions and state transitions.\n- a scriptable control plane, preferably CLI-accessible, for inspection, replay, admin operations, and end-to-end testing without the UI.\n- explicit propagation of real errors to user-facing and operator-facing surfaces instead of generic fallback messages.\n\nFor systems with conversations, jobs, tools, or agents, prefer event models that preserve:\n\n- inputs and outputs,\n- tool calls and tool results,\n- errors and retries,\n- stack traces and failure context,\n- state transitions,\n- timestamps and identifiers needed to reconstruct what happened later.\n\n### Onboarding And Discoverability\n\nAsk what a first-time or returning user sees:\n\n- first-run guidance,\n- empty states,\n- obvious next action,\n- terminology and labels that make sense.\n\n### Risk And Trust Surfaces\n\nAsk where user trust could break:\n\n- silent failure,\n- generic error abstraction that hides the real cause,\n- ambiguous state,\n- lost work,\n- destructive actions,\n- misleading completion signals.\n\n### North Star Product\n\nDescribe what the finished product wants to become if fully realized. Be ambitious. Do not collapse this into the current milestone.\n\n### Current Milestone\n\nDescribe the current coherent slice to build now.\n\nThis is a sequencing choice, not a reduction in imagination.\n\n### Future Product Direction\n\nDescribe what may belong beyond the current implementation plan, so the system keeps a larger product sense without smuggling unfinished required behavior out of the final checklist.\n\n## Important Distinction\n\n- Be expansive in product imagination.\n- Be deliberate in milestone commitment.\n\nYAGNI applies to implementation detail inside the approved milestone. It does not mean shrinking product thinking before the design is even understood.\n# Story-Derived Completeness\n\nUse this reference to expand a sparse product request into a usable v1 by reasoning from user stories and user journeys. This is the primary mechanism for completeness. Archetype references are only a secondary backstop.\n\nThe PM pass should happen first. Use its outputs, especially supporting jobs, state model, operational readiness, observability, onboarding, and trust surfaces, as inputs to the story-derived pass.\n\n## Core Idea\n\nDo not ask only, \"What controls did the user mention?\" Ask, \"What does the user need to do, recover from, revisit, understand, or change?\" The interface and system behaviors should be inferred from those stories.\n\n## Story Categories To Derive\n\nFor app, UI, and product-surface work, derive stories in these categories when relevant:\n\n- primary journey stories,\n- first-run and empty-state stories,\n- return/revisit stories,\n- failure and recovery stories,\n- status-awareness stories,\n- mutation stories,\n- mobile-use stories.\n\n## What Each Category Means\n\n### Primary Journey Stories\n\nWhat is the main task the user is here to complete?\n\nExamples:\n\n- \"As a user, I want to start a conversation with an agent so I can get work done.\"\n- \"As a user, I want to create a task and move it through a board.\"\n\nThese stories usually imply the core surface, main action affordances, and the path from entry to completion.\n\n### First-Run And Empty-State Stories\n\nWhat happens when nothing exists yet?\n\nExamples:\n\n- \"As a first-time user, I want to understand how to begin.\"\n- \"As a user with no conversations yet, I want an obvious way to start one.\"\n\nThese stories imply onboarding affordances, empty states, and entry-point actions.\n\n### Return/Revisit Stories\n\nHow does a user come back later and continue?\n\nExamples:\n\n- \"As a user, I want to find a previous conversation and continue it.\"\n- \"As a user, I want to reopen a document I edited yesterday.\"\n\nThese stories imply lists, recents, navigation hierarchy, and persistence visibility.\n\n### Failure And Recovery Stories\n\nWhat happens when the happy path fails?\n\nExamples:\n\n- \"As a user, I want to know when a request failed and what I can do next.\"\n- \"As a user, I want to retry after a transient failure without losing my work.\"\n\nThese stories imply error notices, retry flows, recovery actions, and backend/frontend coordination.\n\n### Status-Awareness Stories\n\nWhat does the user need to understand about current state?\n\nExamples:\n\n- \"As a user, I want to know whether the agent is still running, waiting for me, or finished.\"\n- \"As a user, I want to know whether my changes were saved.\"\n\nThese stories imply spinners, badges, state labels, progress indicators, and completion signals.\n\nPersistence questions often cut across these categories. When a story depends on preferences, drafts, history, or configuration, ask what must persist, where it should live, and what the user expects to survive across sessions and devices.\n\n### Mutation Stories\n\nHow does a user revise, retry, fork, resend, undo, or continue from earlier state?\n\nExamples:\n\n- \"As a user, I want to edit an earlier prompt and resend from there.\"\n- \"As a user, I want to fork a conversation from a prior turn.\"\n- \"As a user, I want to retry a failed generation.\"\n\nThese stories imply edit/resend flows, truncation behavior, forking, retry actions, and explicit state transitions.\n\n### Mobile-Use Stories\n\nHow does the same job get done on a smaller screen?\n\nExamples:\n\n- \"As a mobile user, I still need to navigate conversations and act on the current thread.\"\n\nThese stories imply drawers, sheets, responsive prioritization, and compact affordances.\n\n## How To Infer Interface And System Behavior\n\nFor each derived story, ask:\n\n1. What interface affordance does this require?\n2. What system behavior does this require?\n3. What visible state does the user need?\n4. What happens when this fails?\n5. What has to be preserved when the user comes back later?\n6. Which settings or preferences does this touch, and should each persist?\n7. Where should that persisted state live, and who owns it?\n8. What happens on reset, migration, logout, or device change?\n\nAlso ask:\n\n9. What product state does this touch?\n10. What observability or status signal should exist?\n11. What would make this feel untrustworthy if it went wrong?\n\n## What Counts As Obvious Vs Debatable\n\n### Obvious Story-Derived Additions\n\nInclude by default when they are clearly implied by the stories:\n\n- lists or history for return/revisit stories,\n- status indicators for status-awareness stories,\n- error notices and retry paths for failure/recovery stories,\n- empty states for first-run stories,\n- edit/retry/resend actions when mutation stories are core to the product's job.\n\n### Debatable Additions\n\nConfirm with the user before locking them in:\n\n- expensive or complex additions,\n- opinionated product bets,\n- large scope expansions,\n- advanced collaboration, analytics, or automation features,\n- behaviors that are plausible but not clearly implied by the approved stories.\n\n## Role Of Archetype References\n\nUse `v1-product-completeness.md` after the story-derived pass:\n\n- as a sanity check,\n- as a prior for familiar product types,\n- as a way to catch obvious omissions.\n\nDo not use archetype references as a replacement for deriving stories from the actual user request.\n# V1 Product Completeness Reference\n\nUse this reference during brainstorming when the request involves a familiar app or product surface. The goal is not to invent extra features. The goal is to avoid shipping a literal shell that lacks the expected surfaces and states needed for a usable v1.\n\nThis file is a sanity check and prior, not the primary source of truth. Derive completeness from user stories and journeys first. Use this reference afterward to catch obvious omissions for familiar archetypes.\n\n## How To Use This Reference\n\n1. Derive user stories and journeys from the request first.\n2. Identify the closest product archetype.\n3. Expand the design into:\n   - explicitly requested capabilities,\n   - baseline expected v1 surfaces and states.\n4. Keep obvious, conventional additions as baseline expected v1 scope.\n5. Confirm non-obvious, expensive, risky, or strongly opinionated additions with the user before locking them in.\n\n## Questions To Ask For Any Archetype\n\n- What is the primary user journey?\n- What does first-run or empty state look like?\n- What does loading, waiting, or in-progress state look like?\n- What does success or completed state look like?\n- What does failure or recovery look like?\n- How does a user find prior work again later?\n- What navigation and hierarchy are expected?\n- What is different on desktop versus mobile?\n\n## AI Chat / Agent Harness\n\n### Baseline Expected V1\n\n- conversation list or history,\n- clear hierarchy when projects or workspaces exist,\n- new conversation action,\n- active conversation view,\n- visible in-progress, waiting, completed, and failed status,\n- input area with obvious send/submit affordance,\n- empty state when no conversation exists,\n- path to resume or revisit prior conversations,\n- mobile-safe navigation pattern such as a drawer or sheet.\n\n### Confirm With User Before Locking In\n\n- advanced filtering or search,\n- rich multi-pane monitoring surfaces,\n- forked conversations,\n- advanced model/provider configuration,\n- collaborative or multi-user features.\n\n## Kanban / Workflow Board\n\n### Baseline Expected V1\n\n- board navigation,\n- column/group structure,\n- visible card status,\n- clear move/add/edit actions,\n- empty state for new boards or columns,\n- loading/error handling,\n- mobile-safe presentation when horizontal space is limited.\n\n### Confirm With User Before Locking In\n\n- swimlanes,\n- automation rules,\n- complex permissions,\n- timeline or reporting views.\n\n## Document Editor\n\n### Baseline Expected V1\n\n- document list or recents,\n- active editing surface,\n- save state or persistence signal,\n- empty state,\n- loading and conflict/error messaging,\n- navigation between documents,\n- mobile editing fallback that still works.\n\n### Confirm With User Before Locking In\n\n- real-time collaboration,\n- comments and suggestions,\n- version history,\n- advanced formatting systems.\n\n## Admin CRUD Surface\n\n### Baseline Expected V1\n\n- list view,\n- detail or edit view,\n- create action,\n- delete/archive handling,\n- empty/loading/error states,\n- basic status messaging,\n- mobile-safe access to actions and details.\n\n### Confirm With User Before Locking In\n\n- bulk operations,\n- role-based permissions,\n- audit history,\n- advanced filters and exports.\n\n## Dashboard / Monitoring Surface\n\n### Baseline Expected V1\n\n- overview surface,\n- clearly labeled primary metrics or status cards,\n- refresh/loading behavior,\n- empty or no-data states,\n- visible alerts/errors,\n- path from summary to underlying item or detail,\n- mobile layout that preserves priority order.\n\n### Confirm With User Before Locking In\n\n- custom report builders,\n- advanced drill-down analytics,\n- notification systems,\n- saved views or personalization.\n# Lifecycle Observability & Resilience Testing\n\n> What we keep getting wrong, why our tests don't catch it, and the discipline that fixes it.\n\nRelated: `docs/architecture/hot-path-responsiveness-and-resource-leaks.md`\ndocuments the May 2026 reload/worker-streaming latency incident and the\nresource-ownership rules for keeping page bootstrap, SSE, and worker stream\npaths responsive while agents are running.\n\n## Multi-runner client lifecycle\n\nEach saved runner profile owns one `RunnerConnection` state machine and at most\none main event stream. A registry starts every profile connection, not only the\nvisible one. Switching the visible workspace changes active managers/query\ncaches and terminal ownership without closing inactive main streams.\n\nEvery bootstrap, snapshot, frame, retry, timer, and mutation checks profile and\nlearned runner identity before applying state. Stored cursors include the\nrunner's stream epoch. `runner.stopping`, `auth.session_revoked`, and\n`stream.resync_required` are typed terminal/control outcomes, not generic\nnetwork failures.\n\nElectron, VS Code, and Capacitor host bridges have bounded stream delivery and\nmust cancel upstream work on overflow. Mobile suspension persists cursors,\ncloses streams, and reconnects on resume; it must not claim background\ndelivery. Platform lifecycle contract tests live under `tests/electron`,\n`tests/vscode`, `tests/mobile`, and `tests/runtime-api`; real two-runner and\neight-runner journeys live under `tests/e2e`.\n\n## Interactive protocol invariant\n\nAn advertised interactive agent capability is operational only when the complete user journey is implemented: receive the request, append it to the unified worker stream, render an actionable control in the main conversation, submit the exact protocol response, and append a terminal outcome before resolving the agent request. Side-panel-only controls do not satisfy this invariant. Every form shape and permission option emitted by a supported adapter must be covered by a finite wire-to-UI regression matrix.\n\nActionability comes only from the live runtime's `pendingElicitations` and\n`pendingPermissions`; an open worker-stream row proves only that a request\nexisted. After reattach or recreation, compare the durable open rows with the\nruntime-owned request ids and append terminal `cancelled` rows for any orphaned\nrequests before publishing the recovered worker. The UI may render the stream\nrows as history, but must never turn them back into response controls.\n\nRelated: `docs/architecture/timing-determinism-audit.md` records the May 20,\n2026 audit of race-prone frontend and control-plane patterns: cached snapshots\npretending to be authoritative, stale async callbacks, timer-owned actions,\ntimestamp-only ordering, and silent wake/lease contention.\n\n## Interrupted-turn recovery invariant\n\nReattaching a provider session restores conversation context; it does not prove\nthat the interrupted turn is running again. If a persisted active direct worker\nis missing after a runner restart and the reattached runtime comes back idle\nwithout a terminal stop reason or pending user input, recovery must explicitly\nsend a continuation prompt, keep the run `running`, and persist the eventual\nturn result. The continuation runs as an owned background conversation task so\nsnapshot bootstrap does not block on the agent turn. A newer user turn may\nsupersede it, but that decision must resolve the recovery incident and emit a\nnamed event.\n\nAn intentional stop or steer abort is a successful ownership handoff, not a\nworker failure. Older initial-turn handlers must recognize the abort and must\nnot emit `run_failed` or overwrite the replacement turn's status. Every queue\nrow claimed by the interrupted delivery must leave `delivering`: close it as\ndelivered if its exact message id reached the transcript, otherwise return it\nto pending. An elicitation answer resumes the turn that already owns the live\nquestion, so it must never wait behind that same turn's per-worker gate; doing\nso is a self-deadlock because the turn cannot finish until the answer arrives.\n\n## Why this document exists\n\nOver a single afternoon we hit three production-class bugs that the existing test\nsuite did not catch and could not have caught:\n\n1. **Session fails to reconnect after a server restart and gives up after the\n   first attempt.** No user-visible message, no telemetry event, no recovery\n   incident — just a silently dead session.\n2. **`DELETE /conversations/:id` fails with a foreign-key constraint** because\n   related rows in dependent tables were not cleared first. The error surfaces\n   as an opaque 500.\n3. **Running plan-improvement agents fails because leftover state blocks the\n   new run.** The block is silent; the user sees nothing happen and assumes the\n   button is broken.\n\nThese are not unrelated bugs. They share a single root cause:\n\n> **The server makes state transitions that nobody observes.** Neither the user,\n> nor the UI, nor any test client can tell what the server decided. So bugs in\n> the *decision* (gave up too early, took the wrong branch, refused silently)\n> are invisible.\n\nThis document records what we learned and the rules we now follow so that this\nclass of bug stops shipping.\n\nRelated incident note: `docs/architecture/supervisor-worker-switching-incident.md`\nrecords the worker-switching failure from session `690d2fa9f2a7`, where\n`awaiting_user` was treated as runnable, Gemini workers were live but persisted\nas `starting`, and duplicate continuation workers were spawned.\n\nRelated direct-control note:\n`docs/architecture/direct-control-session-regressions.md` records the\n`ffb912d4d5d7`, `937e642f3535`, and `734cc38f4d7f` regressions around\nlifecycle noise, stale queued send-now state, missing unified-stream worker\noutput, and permanent \"Thinking...\" indicators.\n\nRelated state-staleness note:\n`docs/architecture/state-staleness-and-session-lifecycle-lessons.md` records\nthe `0785684884fa`, `14260d2a2df0`, `ecea142f7344`, and `18fc406457f3`\nregressions around stale SSE/cache payloads, queued-message races, direct-run\nstatus classification, unread markers, and worker-stream loading.\n\n---\n\n## What we found in the audit\n\nA targeted audit of `src/app/api/events/route.ts` plus the planning, recovery,\nand bridge-client subsystems revealed the structural gaps.\n\n### 1. The SSE stream is a snapshot stream, not an event stream\n\n`/api/events` emits exactly one named event: `update`, whose payload is the\n*entire* current state of the world for the selected run (messages, plans,\nruns, workers, planItems, clarifications, executionEvents, supervisor\ninterventions, queued messages, recovery incidents, review runs/rounds/findings).\n\nThere are no other named events. An observer cannot ask \"did a worker spawn?\"\nor \"was a recovery attempted?\" — only \"is the state now different?\" To answer\nthe first kind of question the client must **diff snapshots**, which is:\n\n- **Ambiguous** — many transitions produce identical visible state (a worker\n  that was reattached and a worker that was recreated both end up as\n  `status=\"running\"`).\n- **Lossy** — fast transitions can be coalesced inside a single snapshot\n  interval and disappear entirely. (e.g. `starting → running → completed`\n  inside one 15s window.)\n- **Forced to re-derive server decisions** — the client has to reconstruct\n  *why* state changed, which requires duplicating server logic.\n\n### 2. There is no event ID and no resume\n\nThe SSE frames have no `id:` field. There is no `Last-Event-ID` handling on the\nserver. A client that disconnects and reconnects gets a fresh snapshot and has\n*no way to know* whether events fired during the gap. For chaos testing (where\nwe deliberately drop the connection) this is fatal — we cannot assert\n\"between t0 and t1 the server emitted [X, Y, Z]\" because the wire format does\nnot carry that information.\n\nThere is a `?snapshot=1` bootstrap endpoint, which is good. There is no\n\"give me the events I missed since id N\" endpoint, which is the gap.\n\n### 3. Several decisions are invisible to the wire\n\nConcrete state transitions that **the server makes** but **never tells anyone\nabout**:\n\n| Transition | Where it happens | Wire visibility |\n|---|---|---|\n| Worker spawned | `bridge-client/index.ts spawnAgent` | None — inferred from a row appearing |\n| Worker reattached vs recreated after restart | `restart-control.ts` / recovery path | **None** — both look like `status=\"running\"` |\n| Recovery gave up after N attempts | `recoveryIncidents.autoAttemptCount` increments | Partial — count is visible but there is no terminal \"abandoned\" state |\n| Plan readiness reached | `derivePlanningStatus()` — runs **client-side** | **None** — it is a client-side derivation, not a server event |\n| Plan-improvement blocked by leftover state | `planning/review.ts` early-return | **None** — silent refusal |\n| Conversation delete FK violation | `/api/conversations/[id]` DELETE | None as a typed event — only a 500 response |\n| Message ack / persisted / streaming chunk | `conversations/send-message.ts` | Partial — only the resulting queued-message status |\n\nEvery row in this table is a bug class waiting to ship. If the server can\nsilently decide \"I gave up\", \"I refused\", \"I took branch B not A\", and the\ntest harness has no way to assert which branch it took, the test harness is\nstructurally incapable of catching the bug.\n\n### 4. The user-facing surface mirrors the test-facing surface\n\nThe reason \"leftover state blocked the run\" did not show a user message is the\n**same reason** no test could detect it: there was no event for it. Make the\nstate transitions observable, and both the UI fix and the test coverage\nfollow. Conversely, if a transition is observable only by re-deriving from a\nsnapshot diff, both the UI and tests will be brittle in the same ways.\n\nThis is the central insight: **observability for users and observability for\ntests are the same problem**.\n\n### 5. Playwright e2e is the wrong layer for resilience\n\nThe existing `tests/e2e/*.spec.ts` files boot Chromium. They are slow (tens of\nseconds per scenario), flaky against real subprocess restarts, and they\nexercise UI rendering rather than control-plane decisions. The bugs we are\nhitting are not UI bugs — they are control-plane bugs. We should be hitting\nHTTP/SSE directly, in Node, with no browser. The UI is one consumer of the\ncontrol plane; the chaos harness is another.\n\n---\n\n## The rules we now follow\n\nThese are not suggestions. They are the discipline that makes this class of\nbug stop happening.\n\n### Rule 1: every server-side state transition emits a named event\n\nIf the server makes a decision — spawn, reattach, recreate, give up, refuse,\ndelete, fail — that decision is published on the event stream with a\n**named event type**, not just a state delta. Examples:\n\n- `worker.spawned`, `worker.status` (with `prev` and `next`), `worker.terminal`\n- `worker.reattached`, `worker.recreated`\n- `plan.ready`, `plan.review.started`, `plan.review.finished`,\n  `plan.review.blocked` (with a `reason`)\n- `recovery.opened`, `recovery.attempt`, `recovery.gave_up`, `recovery.resolved`\n- `conversation.deleted`, `conversation.delete_failed` (with FK details)\n- `error.surfaced` — the catch-all for anything the user should know about\n\n**Rule of thumb:** if a server method has an early-return, a try/catch that\nswallows, or a branch that depends on a state check, it emits an event.\n\"Silent\" is a code smell.\n\n### Rule 2: errors that affect the user are events, not just response codes\n\nA 500 with a body is not enough. A 200 that quietly did nothing is worse. Every\nuser-relevant failure publishes `error.surfaced` with a stable code, a\nhuman-readable message, and the relevant run/worker/conversation ids. The UI's\njob is to render `error.surfaced` events into toasts/banners. The test\nharness's job is to assert that the expected `error.surfaced` event fired.\n\nA failure that does not produce an `error.surfaced` event is a bug — even if\nthe underlying behaviour is technically correct — because it is invisible to\nthe user and to the tests.\n\nThe event carries the stable id of the subject that failed. Conversation and\nworker failures use `runId`, `conversationId`, or `workerId`; account-only\nsettings failures use `accountId` rather than pretending an account is a\nconversation.\n\n### Rule 3: the event stream is SSE-spec compliant\n\n- Every frame has an `id:` field. IDs are monotonic.\n- The server keeps a bounded **ring buffer** (≥ 500 entries) of recent events.\n- The server honours `Last-Event-ID` on reconnect: it replays everything after\n  that id from the buffer, then resumes live streaming.\n- If the requested id has fallen out of the buffer, the server emits a\n  `stream.resync_required` event so the client knows to re-bootstrap from\n  `?snapshot=1` rather than silently miss events.\n\nThis is the SSE spec. We were not following it. We are now.\n\n### Rule 4: named events alongside, not instead of, snapshots\n\nThe existing `update` snapshot event stays. The UI keeps consuming it. The new\nnamed events are *additional* frames on the same stream. Clients that don't\ncare about a given event type ignore it. There is no `?test=1` flag, no\nseparate test endpoint, no parallel surface that can drift.\n\nIf a transition is worth emitting for tests, it is worth emitting in\nproduction. Anything else creates a second observability surface that will\ndiverge from reality.\n\n### Rule 5: chaos testing belongs in the client, not the server\n\nWe do **not** add fault-injection code paths to the server. We do not add a\n\"random failure\" mode. The server stays clean. Chaos lives in the test\nclient as middleware:\n\n- An SSE wrapper that can drop the connection on command.\n- A `fetch` wrapper that can fail a configurable percentage of requests.\n- A harness that can SIGKILL the spawned server process and restart it.\n\nFaults are seeded for reproducibility. Every failure has a seed; replaying\nthe seed reproduces the failure.\n\n### Rule 6: tests assert on event sequences, not on rendered state\n\nA scenario test reads like a transcript:\n\n```\nexpect(events).toHaveEmitted(\"worker.spawned\", { runId });\nexpect(events).toHaveEmitted(\"worker.status\", { next: \"running\" });\nchaos.killServer();\nchaos.restartServer();\nexpect(events).toHaveEmitted(\"worker.reattached\", { runId });\n// not \"worker.recreated\", not absence — explicitly reattached\n```\n\n### Rule 7: cleanup waits for server-owned background work\n\nLifecycle scenarios may call routes that intentionally return before a direct\nworker turn finishes. That is fine for product latency, but it is not fine for\ntest cleanup to delete `OMNIHARNESS_ROOT` while those server-owned tasks are\nstill writing worker JSONL, recovery rows, or named events.\n\nRequired shape:\n\n- Fire-and-forget direct/planning turns are registered as background\n  conversation tasks.\n- The lifecycle harness waits for registered background tasks before deleting\n  its temp root.\n- A timeout while waiting is a test failure, not harmless stderr after the test\n  already passed.\n- Do not paper over storage `ENOENT` from cleanup races by retrying file writes;\n  the ownership bug is untracked background work.\n\nProduction deletion follows the same ownership rule. A conversation delete\ninstalls a run-scoped deletion fence before cancelling workers or removing\nrows. Initial worker startup checks that fence both before and after spawn, so\na worker that finishes spawning during deletion is cancelled before its prompt\ncan run. The fence remains until every tracked background task for the run has\nsettled; a one-shot best-effort cancel is not sufficient.\n\nAsserting on rendered DOM state is the wrong granularity. We assert on the\nserver's *decisions*, which are now first-class events.\n\n### Rule 7: the test harness runs in isolation\n\nA separate Node process. A temporary `OMNI_HOME`. A fresh sqlite. A dedicated\nport. No collision with the real dev server. Tear it all down on exit.\nScenarios can run in parallel because each one owns its own server.\n\nThis isolation must not trust inherited runner environment. Agents spawned by\nOmniHarness may inherit server control variables, so Vitest always replaces\n`OMNIHARNESS_ROOT` with a newly created temporary root. Database startup in a\nrecognized test process refuses any non-temporary root, and ACP agent children\nmust not receive `OMNIHARNESS_ROOT`, `OMNIHARNESS_INSTANCE`, bridge addresses,\nor runner addresses. A test failure is always preferable to opening live data.\n\n### Rule 8: snapshot bootstrap, event tail thereafter\n\nEvery harness client starts by calling `GET /api/events?snapshot=1` for state\ntruth, then opens the SSE stream with `Last-Event-ID` set to the id at\nsnapshot time. This is the canonical pattern. The UI should do this too;\nright now it doesn't, which is a latent bug.\n\n### Rule 9: snapshots declare scoped completeness\n\nAn event snapshot may be a full replacement for a selected run's transcript,\nor it may be only a partial update. The payload must say which one it is.\n\n`messageScope` carries the contract:\n\n- `runIds`: the conversation run ids whose message set the payload is talking\n  about. For promoted implementation runs this includes the parent planning\n  run plus the selected implementation run.\n- `complete`: `true` means the payload contains the complete message set for\n  those run ids, so absent messages may be removed locally. `false` or a\n  missing scope means the payload is additive and must not erase durable\n  messages already known to the client.\n\nNever infer completeness from \"this snapshot contains a message for that run.\"\nThat was the race behind the disappearing preflight confirmation: a partial\nlive update containing only the user checkpoint looked authoritative enough to\nerase the supervisor confirmation from frontend state.\n\n`snapshotScope` extends the same idea to bounded streams that live in\nartifact storage (execution events, supervisor interventions, planning review\nfindings). Each entry reports `{limit, complete, oldestCreatedAt}`. Clients\nthat want older records keep a cursor (`oldestCreatedAt`) and request a\ndeeper window; clients that just need the head trust the snapshot and stop.\n\n### Append-only artifact streams\n\nThe bulky payload fields of these record families now live in JSONL files\nunder `<projectPath>/.omniharness/run-data/<runId>/`, not in SQLite:\n\n- `execution_events.details` → `execution-events.jsonl`\n- `supervisor_interventions.{prompt,summary}` → `supervisor-interventions.jsonl`\n- `planning_review_findings.{details,recommendation}` → `planning-review-findings.jsonl`\n- `worker_entries` (per-worker) → `workers/<workerId>.jsonl`\n\nThe metadata layer (`artifact_streams`) keeps a row per (runId, kind, ownerId)\nwith `latest_seq`, `latest_record_id`, `compacted_at`, and the resolved\n`project_path` + `relative_root_path`. Each domain row keeps a content hash\nand a short preview so list views don't need to read the artifact. Failure\nmodes surface through named events:\n\n- `artifact.append_failed` — JSONL write or lock acquisition failed.\n- `artifact.metadata_mismatch` — `latest_seq` and the file tail disagree.\n- `artifact.compaction_failed` / `artifact.compaction_completed` — terminal-run\n  sweep result.\n- `artifact.payload_missing` — domain row points at an artifact line that\n  isn't on disk (run `pnpm exec tsx scripts/artifact-repair.ts`).\n- `artifact.backfill_failed` — emitted by the migration script.\n\nSnapshot readers tolerate hydration failures: rows render from the preview\ncolumn and emit `artifact.payload_missing` so an operator can repair.\n\n### Rule 10: `awaiting_user` implies a visible question\n\nA selected run with `status=\"awaiting_user\"` must include a visible supervisor\nmessage with kind `clarification` or `implementation_confirmation`. The\ntransition that creates this state must persist the clarification row, the\nsupervisor message, the run status, and the lifecycle event before notifying\nSSE clients.\n\nIf a snapshot violates this invariant, it surfaces a `Lifecycle` frontend\nerror instead of leaving the UI stuck on \"Loading Omni's question\". The error\nis not a substitute for the invariant; it is the safety rail that makes any\nfuture violation observable.\n\n### Rule 11: terminal user actions retire recovery ownership first\n\nA user stop is not complete when only `runs.status` and `workers.status` are\nterminal. Before publishing the terminal run state, the server synchronously\ncancels volatile and durable wakes, clears wake leases, and resolves every open\nrecovery incident with its normal named event. Otherwise a later sync or timer\nstill owns permission to reactivate the run. Once cancelled, the run rejects\nlate live-worker snapshots from the interrupted turn even when the provider\ncontinues reporting `working` briefly.\n\nConversation deletion follows the same ownership rule. Before deleting a\nworker or run, enumerate and delete every row that references it, including\ncredential allocations and token usage. Route tests must insert a row in each\nworker-referencing table so new foreign keys cannot silently make deletion\npartial or impossible.\n\n### Rule 12: dependent selections cross the boundary together\n\nWorker type, account, model, and effort are one compound composer selection.\nThe value shown in the composer, sent to prewarm, and submitted to conversation\ncreation must come from the same compatibility resolution. A raw account id\nfrom a previously selected worker type is not an explicit preference for the\nnew worker.\n\nRun selection restores the whole compound selection, including the account.\nOptimistic run snapshots must carry the same selection fields as the durable\nrun so selecting a newly created conversation cannot replace an explicit\naccount with `auto` before the authoritative snapshot arrives.\n\nThe server still validates every explicit account selection, but it does so\nbefore creating plan, run, worker, message, or artifact state. Rejections use\nthe original client error status and emit `error.surfaced` with a stable code;\nthey never leave a partially created conversation for snapshots to discover.\n\n---\n\n## Anti-patterns to refuse on sight\n\nThese are the specific shapes of code that produce the bugs in this document.\nReviewers should reject these on sight.\n\n1. **Silent early returns in server methods.**\n   ```ts\n   if (hasLeftoverState) return; // ← BUG\n   ```\n   If a method refuses to proceed, it emits an event explaining why.\n\n2. **try/catch that swallows.**\n   ```ts\n   try { await doThing(); } catch { /* ignore */ } // ← BUG\n   ```\n   Either rethrow, or emit `error.surfaced` with the context. Never both\n   silent and continuing.\n\n3. **Client-side derivation of server decisions.**\n   If the server decided something (plan is ready, recovery gave up), the\n   server emits the event. The client does not re-derive it. If the client\n   is computing `derivePlanningStatus()` from raw fields, that is a missing\n   server event.\n\n4. **SSE frames without `id:`.**\n   Every frame has an id. No exceptions.\n\n5. **State diffs as the primary observability mechanism.**\n   \"The UI will figure out what happened by diffing snapshots\" is not an\n   observability strategy. Name the event.\n\n6. **Fault injection inside production code paths.**\n   `if (process.env.CHAOS) throw new Error(\"boom\")` does not appear in\n   server code. Chaos is a client-side concern.\n\n7. **Tests that boot Chromium to assert on control-plane behaviour.**\n   Use the HTTP/SSE harness. Reserve Playwright for actual UI behaviour\n   (rendering, input handling, layout).\n\n8. **DELETE handlers that don't enumerate dependent rows.**\n   If a row has foreign-key dependents, the DELETE handler either deletes\n   them in a transaction or emits `conversation.delete_failed` with the\n   blocking table/row. Never let the FK error reach the client as a raw 500.\n\n9. **Recovery loops without a terminal state.**\n   If `autoAttemptCount` keeps incrementing without an `abandoned` or\n   `gave_up` terminal event, there is no way for anyone — user, UI, test —\n   to know the system has given up. Always have a terminal state, and\n   always emit it.\n\n10. **Test-only code paths.**\n    `if (process.env.NODE_ENV === \"test\")` in production code is a bug. The\n    test harness should be able to drive production code paths from the\n    outside. The only exception is the dev-only `/api/events/log` polling\n    endpoint, which is build-stripped, has no behavioural effect, and exists\n    purely as a read-side affordance.\n\n---\n\n## How to add a new server-side decision\n\nThe checklist, every time:\n\n1. Pick the event name. Format: `domain.verb` (`worker.spawned`,\n   `plan.review.blocked`). Past tense for things that happened, no\n   \"will/should\".\n2. Define the payload shape in `src/server/events/named-events.ts` (TypeScript\n   union, exported).\n3. At the point of decision, call `emitNamedEvent(\"domain.verb\", payload)`.\n   The emitter writes to the ring buffer and triggers the SSE notifier.\n4. If the decision is a *failure* the user should see, also emit\n   `error.surfaced` with a stable code and human message.\n"
  }
]
````

<!-- codex-event:{"kind":"final","timestamp":"2026-08-05T09:05:45.713Z","phase":"final_answer"} -->
## Codex final · 2026-08-05T09:05:45.713Z

The conversation is intact. Claude authentication expired, and OmniHarness incorrectly turned that into:

> Recover conversation → Ask failed: Authentication required

It should instead show “Claude sign-in required,” stop automatic recovery, and preserve a one-click path back to the conversation.

The reliable design is:

- Emit a typed `worker.auth_required` event with the run, worker, and account IDs.
- Move the run to `needs_recovery`, not generic `failed`.
- Show a “Sign in to Claude” action directly in the conversation.
- Start Claude’s supported remote login process on the runner.
- Open its authorization URL in the OmniHarness browser.
- Ask the user to paste the returned authorization code into OmniHarness.
- Send that code to the waiting Claude process on the runner.
- Verify authentication, refresh account status, then offer “Resume conversation.”
- Never store or log the authorization code, OAuth URL state, or tokens.

I confirmed the installed Claude Code flow: it prints an authorization URL and then waits at `Paste code here if prompted >`. Anthropic documents the same URL-and-code approach for remote/headless authentication. [Anthropic authentication documentation](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/cli-sdks-libraries/cli/authentication)

A completely automatic remote callback is not possible from an ordinary browser: Anthropic’s callback page is on another origin, so OmniHarness cannot read the returned code, and a `localhost` callback would target the browser’s machine rather than the runner. Zero-paste automation would require a native helper, browser extension, or an Anthropic-supported redirect URI.

My recommendation is to build the secure cross-machine flow now with one paste step, while keeping the authentication state machine ready for a future native callback relay.

Should I proceed with that design?

<!-- /codex-event -->

## Visible reasoning summary · 2026-08-05T09:53:55.403Z

**Recommending extension-based OAuth callback capture**

<!-- /codex-event -->

<!-- codex-event:{"kind":"final","timestamp":"2026-08-05T09:54:15.505Z","phase":"final_answer"} -->
## Codex final · 2026-08-05T09:54:15.505Z

By “OmniHarness browser,” I meant whichever browser is displaying OmniHarness—not a separate built-in browser.

- Web app in Chrome/Safari/Firefox: sign-in opens a new tab or popup in that browser.
- Electron app: the codebase opens links in the system’s default browser.
- VS Code extension: VS Code opens the system’s default browser.
- Mobile app: it uses the platform’s external-browser handler.

This is unrelated to Codex’s in-app browser tool.

### Browser-extension approach

A small Chrome/Edge extension is the cleanest zero-paste option for web users:

1. You click “Sign in to Claude.”
2. OmniHarness starts `claude auth login` on the runner.
3. OmniHarness opens Claude’s authorization page in Chrome.
4. You authenticate and approve access.
5. Anthropic redirects to its code callback page.
6. The extension detects that specific callback page and extracts the returned authorization code.
7. It securely sends the code to the originating OmniHarness tab.
8. OmniHarness forwards it to the waiting Claude process on the runner.
9. Claude stores its credentials and OmniHarness resumes the conversation.

The extension would only activate for an explicit, short-lived OmniHarness login attempt. It should use:

- An unpredictable operation ID and one-use secret.
- Exact OAuth `state` matching.
- A five-minute expiration.
- Permission only for Anthropic’s callback page.
- No persistent access to Claude tokens.
- No logging of URLs, codes, or credentials.

The disadvantages are installation, browser-specific packaging, and limited mobile/Safari support. It would also depend somewhat on Anthropic’s callback-page structure unless the authorization code is available directly in the final URL.

### Native-helper approach

There are two possible native versions.

1. **Electron-controlled authorization window**

   OmniHarness opens Claude authentication in a dedicated Electron browser window, observes the final callback navigation, obtains the code, and forwards it to the runner.

   This is integrated and needs no extension, but the authorization window may not share your normal browser’s login cookies. You might need to sign into Claude again there, and some providers object to embedded OAuth windows.

2. **Local callback relay**

   A small OmniHarness helper runs on the computer containing your browser and listens on `localhost`. The runner starts Claude’s local-callback OAuth flow. After authentication:

   - The browser sends the callback to the local helper.
   - The helper forwards the exact callback through the authenticated OmniHarness connection.
   - The runner delivers it to the waiting Claude process.

   This is technically cleaner than scraping a webpage, but it relies on Claude continuing to support its localhost callback flow and requires installing and running a local companion process. The Electron app could provide this helper automatically.

The repository already contains an `omniharness://authorize` deep-link mechanism for OmniHarness’s own authorization flow. Unfortunately, Anthropic cannot simply redirect to that address because OAuth redirect destinations must be registered by Anthropic.

### My recommendation

For users accessing OmniHarness through Chrome, build the companion extension. It gives the desired experience:

> Click Sign in → approve Claude → conversation resumes.

For the packaged Electron app, add native handling afterward. Both should sit on top of the same server-side login state machine, with the supported copy-and-paste flow as a fallback when neither helper is available.

<!-- /codex-event -->

## Visible reasoning summary · 2026-08-05T10:08:32.718Z

<!-- codex-event:{"kind":"final","timestamp":"2026-08-05T10:08:45.323Z","phase":"final_answer"} -->
## Codex final · 2026-08-05T10:08:45.323Z

Not from the normal OmniHarness webpage. Browser security deliberately prevents JavaScript on `omniharness.example` from reading or modifying a page on `platform.claude.com`.

These approaches will not bypass that:

- Opening Claude with `window.open()` and reading the popup.
- Embedding Claude in an iframe.
- Injecting a `<script>` from the OmniHarness tab.
- Using a service worker or polling the popup URL.
- Reading Claude’s DOM or clipboard from the OmniHarness origin.

The same-origin policy blocks all of them. If ordinary websites could inject JavaScript into login pages, any malicious site could steal authentication codes.

JavaScript injection becomes possible only when something has higher browser privileges:

- **Chrome extension:** a content script runs specifically on Anthropic’s callback page and returns the code.
- **Electron app:** open authentication inside an OmniHarness-owned browser window and inspect the final navigation or page.
- **Local browser helper:** connect through Chrome’s debugging protocol and inspect the callback tab. This requires launching Chrome with debugging enabled and grants dangerously broad access.
- **Userscript/bookmarklet:** technically possible, but it still requires installation or a manual click and is less controlled than an extension.

The Electron approach does not necessarily need injected JavaScript. If the authorization code appears in the callback URL, Electron can capture it during navigation. If it only appears in the page content, Electron can read that one page after verifying the exact Anthropic origin and OAuth state.

One important correction: OmniHarness currently opens external links from Electron in your normal system browser. It cannot inject anything into that browser. For native automatic login, we would need a dedicated, isolated Electron authorization window instead.

So the practical choices are:

- Plain web OmniHarness: extension for full automation.
- Electron OmniHarness: dedicated authorization window, no extension.
- No helper at all: supported one-time code paste.

A “Paste from clipboard” button could reduce the fallback to one click, but OmniHarness still cannot copy the code out of Anthropic’s page itself.

<!-- /codex-event -->

## Codex progress · 2026-08-05T10:40:58.177Z

I’ll use that as the approved first version: OmniHarness opens Claude’s supported authorization page in the user’s normal system browser, shows a secure code field, sends the pasted code to the waiting Claude process on the runner, verifies the login, and then resumes the conversation.

The product-design workflow requires me to record the agreed behavior and security boundaries before changing authentication code. I’m writing that short specification now; it will not create a branch or touch the existing unrelated changes.

modifiedPath: string; title?: string }): Promise<{ ok: true }>;\n  };\n}\nimport type { RuntimeAPIs, RuntimeSubscription } from \"./types\";\nimport { createFetchRuntimeRequest } from \"./request\";\nimport {\n  normalizeRuntimeStreamEvent,\n  type RuntimeStreamEvent,\n} from \"./stream\";\nimport { createRuntimeDomains } from \"./domains\";\n\nexport interface WebRuntimeApiOptions {\n  baseUrl?: string;\n  fetchImpl?: typeof fetch;\n  EventSourceImpl?: EventSourceConstructor;\n  bearerToken?: string | (() => string | null);\n}\n\ntype RuntimeEventSource = {\n  close(): void;\n  readyState?: number;\n  onopen?: (() => void) | null;\n  addEventListener(type: string, listener: (event: RuntimeStreamEvent) => void): void;\n};\n\ntype EventSourceConstructor = new (url: string) => RuntimeEventSource;\n\nfunction joinUrl(baseUrl: string, path: string) {\n  if (!baseUrl) return path;\n  return `${baseUrl.replace(/\\/$/, \"\")}${path}`;\n}\n\nexport function createWebRuntimeAPIs(options: WebRuntimeApiOptions = {}): RuntimeAPIs {\n  const fetchImpl = options.fetchImpl\n    ?? (typeof fetch !== \"undefined\" ? fetch : null);\n  if (!fetchImpl) {\n    throw new TypeError(\"A fetch implementation is required.\");\n  }\n  const baseUrl = options.baseUrl ?? \"\";\n  const EventSourceCtor: EventSourceConstructor | null = options.EventSourceImpl\n    ?? (typeof EventSource !== \"undefined\"\n      ? EventSource as unknown as EventSourceConstructor\n      : null);\n\n  const request = createFetchRuntimeRequest({\n    baseUrl,\n    fetchImpl,\n    surface: \"web\",\n    bearerToken: options.bearerToken,\n  });\n\n  const domains = createRuntimeDomains({\n    request,\n    openEvents(path, input, handlers): RuntimeSubscription {\n      if (!EventSourceCtor) {\n        handlers.onError?.({\n          code: \"runtime.events_unavailable\",\n          message: \"Runtime event streaming requires EventSource support.\",\n          surface: \"web\",\n        });\n        return { close() {} };\n      }\n\n      let source: RuntimeEventSource | null = null;\n      let closed = false;\n      const emit = (event: RuntimeStreamEvent) => {\n        handlers.onEvent(normalizeRuntimeStreamEvent(event));\n      };\n      const connect = async () => {\n        let ticket: string | null = null;\n        if (options.bearerToken) {\n          const issued = await request(\"POST\", \"/api/auth/stream-ticket\", {\n            body: { path: new URL(path, \"http://runtime.local\").pathname },\n          }) as { ticket: string };\n          ticket = issued.ticket;\n        }\n        if (closed) {\n          return;\n        }\n        const query = new URLSearchParams();\n        if (ticket) {\n          query.set(\"ticket\", ticket);\n        }\n        if (input.lastEventId) {\n          query.set(\"cursor\", input.lastEventId);\n        }\n        const separator = path.includes(\"?\") ? \"&\" : \"?\";\n        const suffix = query.size > 0 ? `${separator}${query.toString()}` : \"\";\n        const nextSource = new EventSourceCtor(\n          joinUrl(baseUrl, `${path}${suffix}`),\n        );\n        source = nextSource;\n        nextSource.onopen = () => handlers.onOpen?.();\n        nextSource.addEventListener(\"message\", emit);\n        nextSource.addEventListener(\"update\", emit);\n        nextSource.addEventListener(\"data\", emit);\n        nextSource.addEventListener(\"exit\", emit);\n        nextSource.addEventListener(\"update_error\", emit);\n        nextSource.addEventListener(\"worker.entry_appended\", emit);\n        nextSource.addEventListener(\"stream.resync_required\", emit);\n        nextSource.addEventListener(\"auth.session_revoked\", emit);\n        nextSource.addEventListener(\"runner.stopping\", emit);\n        nextSource.addEventListener(\"runner.renamed\", emit);\n        nextSource.addEventListener(\"runner.rekeyed\", emit);\n        nextSource.addEventListener(\"error\", () => {\n          if (closed || source !== nextSource) {\n            return;\n          }\n          if (nextSource.readyState !== 2) {\n            handlers.onError?.({\n              code: \"runtime.events_reconnecting\",\n              message: \"Event stream is reconnecting.\",\n              surface: \"web\",\n            });\n            return;\n          }\n          nextSource.close();\n          source = null;\n          handlers.onError?.({\n            code: \"runtime.events_failed\",\n            message: \"Event stream failed.\",\n            surface: \"web\",\n          });\n        });\n      };\n      void connect().catch((error: unknown) => {\n        if (closed) {\n          return;\n        }\n        handlers.onError?.({\n          code: \"runtime.stream_ticket_failed\",\n          message: error instanceof Error ? error.message : String(error),\n          surface: \"web\",\n        });\n      });\n      return {\n        close: () => {\n          closed = true;\n          source?.close();\n        },\n      };\n    },\n  });\n  return {\n    runtime: {\n      surface: \"web\",\n      label: \"Web\",\n      supportsNativeNotifications: false,\n      supportsEditorActions: false,\n    },\n    ...domains,\n  };\n}\n"

## Tool call: exec · 2026-08-05T10:42:28.851Z

```json
const patch = "*** Begin Patch\n*** Add File: /Users/masterman/NLP/omniharness/docs/superpowers/specs/2026-08-05-claude-authentication-recovery-design.md\n+# Claude Authentication Recovery\n+\n+**Date:** 2026-08-05\n+\n+## Goal\n+\n+When a Claude worker cannot answer because its selected account is no longer authenticated, OmniHarness must identify the failure as an account problem, preserve the conversation, and guide the user through Claude's supported remote login flow.\n+\n+The first milestone opens Claude's authorization URL in the user's normal system browser and asks the user to paste the returned authorization code into OmniHarness. The waiting login process runs on the same runner and under the same account environment as the Claude worker, so successful login repairs the credential that the conversation will actually use.\n+\n+Browser extensions, injected callback scripts, native callback relays, and automatic code capture are explicitly deferred.\n+\n+## First User and Core Job\n+\n+The first user is the person running coding-agent conversations through OmniHarness, possibly with the interface and runner on different machines.\n+\n+The core job is: recover a Claude conversation after authentication expires without losing the conversation, opening a terminal manually, or guessing which runner/account must be repaired.\n+\n+Supporting jobs are:\n+\n+- understand that Claude authentication failed rather than the conversation being corrupted;\n+- sign in to the exact account selected by the failed worker;\n+- see whether login is starting, waiting for a code, verifying, completed, expired, cancelled, or failed;\n+- retry or resume the failed conversation after authentication succeeds;\n+- return after a page reload without mistaking stale client state for a live login process;\n+- inspect the server's decisions and errors through named events and tests.\n+\n+## Confirmed Failure\n+\n+Session `cf466dfc2492` proves the current problem:\n+\n+- the run and unified worker transcript are intact;\n+- the saved Claude session was found and reattached;\n+- the first prompt failed with `Authentication required`;\n+- the UI rendered the mutation error as `Recover conversation — Ask failed: Authentication required`;\n+- no recovery incident or account-focused error explained that signing in was the required action.\n+\n+This is both an error-classification failure and a missing recovery journey.\n+\n+## User Journey\n+\n+1. A Claude prompt or recovery attempt returns a recognized authentication failure.\n+2. The server records the run as needing recovery, associates the failure with the selected account, emits `account.login_required`, and emits `error.surfaced` with stable code `account.login_required`.\n+3. The conversation renders a Claude sign-in notice instead of a generic Ask/Recover error. The notice identifies the account label and preserves the failed conversation action.\n+4. The user selects **Sign in to Claude**.\n+5. The runner starts one bounded Claude login operation for that account using Claude's subscription login command and the selected account's resolved environment.\n+6. When the login process prints a valid Anthropic authorization URL, OmniHarness opens it:\n+   - web: a new tab/window in the browser displaying OmniHarness;\n+   - Electron: the system default browser through the native bridge;\n+   - VS Code: the system default browser through `vscode.env.openExternal`;\n+   - mobile: the platform external-browser handler.\n+7. OmniHarness shows the account, the authorization host, a single-line authorization-code field, **Complete sign-in**, **Open link again**, and **Cancel**.\n+8. The user completes Claude authentication in the browser, copies the returned code, pastes it into OmniHarness, and selects **Complete sign-in**.\n+9. The runner writes the code only to the waiting login process's stdin and waits for a bounded terminal result.\n+10. On success, the server runs an authentication-status check under the same account environment. Process exit alone is not sufficient proof.\n+11. The account status and worker catalog refresh. The conversation notice changes to **Claude is signed in** and offers **Resume conversation**. It must not automatically submit an old prompt unless the recovery operation still owns the currently selected failed action.\n+12. Resume uses the existing saved Claude session and unified transcript recovery path.\n+\n+If the popup is blocked, OmniHarness keeps the login operation alive and presents an ordinary clickable authorization link. If the user closes the browser tab, the operation remains in `awaiting_code` until cancelled or expired.\n+\n+## State and Ownership\n+\n+The server owns the authoritative login state. A dedicated `ClaudeLoginManager` owns at most one live login operation per runner/account pair:\n+\n+`starting -> awaiting_code -> verifying -> authenticated`\n+\n+Terminal alternatives are:\n+\n+- `failed`\n+- `cancelled`\n+- `expired`\n+\n+Each operation has a random `operationId`, `accountId`, creation time, expiration time, process ownership, and optional associated `runId`/`workerId`. The authorization URL and code are volatile secrets and are never written to SQLite, JSONL, logs, execution events, error payloads, or browser storage.\n+\n+The client uses a global manager keyed by runner identity and `operationId`. Async start, submit, cancel, status, and event results must prove that the runner identity, account ID, and operation ID still match before changing visible state. Cached client state may render a preview but cannot claim that an operation is live after reload; the server snapshot/status response is authoritative.\n+\n+Closing the dialog does not silently cancel the operation. Explicit **Cancel** does. Expiration terminates the process and requires starting a fresh OAuth attempt.\n+\n+## Server Boundaries\n+\n+The login service is account-specific, not a general remote-shell endpoint.\n+\n+- It accepts only an enabled Claude `local_session` account.\n+- It resolves credentials and environment through the same account resolver used by worker launch.\n+- It spawns a fixed argument vector for Claude subscription login; the browser or client cannot supply a command, executable, arguments, working directory, or environment.\n+- It uses a PTY because Claude's login flow is interactive.\n+- It parses bounded terminal output for an HTTPS authorization URL whose hostname is on the explicit Anthropic allowlist.\n+- It strips terminal control sequences before parsing or returning display text.\n+- It accepts one bounded code value, rejects control characters and oversized input, appends the terminal newline server-side, and never echoes the code.\n+- It has startup, awaiting-code, verification, and total-operation timeouts.\n+- It kills and releases the PTY on success, failure, cancellation, expiration, account deletion, or runner shutdown.\n+- It never reuses the general-purpose terminal routes or exposes an arbitrary terminal ID to this UI.\n+\n+Proposed authenticated routes:\n+\n+- `POST /api/accounts/:id/claude-login` — begin or return the account's current live operation;\n+- `GET /api/accounts/:id/claude-login` — authoritative operation status without secrets after the initial URL delivery;\n+- `POST /api/accounts/:id/claude-login/code` — submit `{ operationId, code }`;\n+- `DELETE /api/accounts/:id/claude-login` — cancel `{ operationId }`.\n+\n+All state-changing requests require an authenticated OmniHarness session and same-origin enforcement. Multi-runner calls go through the existing runtime API for the selected runner.\n+\n+## URL Handling\n+\n+Opening the browser is a client-side action caused by the user's click.\n+\n+For the plain web runtime, the click opens a blank named window synchronously before waiting for the server to return the authorization URL; this avoids popup blockers. Once the URL arrives and passes client-side HTTPS/hostname validation, the blank window navigates to it. Failure closes the blank window when possible and keeps an ordinary link available.\n+\n+Native runtimes use their existing `openExternal` capability. No runtime injects JavaScript into Anthropic pages, reads their DOM, or attempts to intercept the callback in this milestone.\n+\n+## Error Classification and Conversation Recovery\n+\n+Claude authentication failures include the provider's exact forms such as `Authentication required`, `not authenticated`, revoked/expired OAuth tokens, and failed authentication responses. Classification happens at the server boundary where the worker prompt fails.\n+\n+For a direct conversation:\n+\n+- persist the worker terminal error;\n+- set the run to `needs_recovery` with the original provider error preserved;\n+- open or update an authentication recovery incident associated with the run, worker, and account;\n+- do not keep auto-resuming against the same unauthenticated account;\n+- emit the account and error events below;\n+- return a structured application error carrying `accountId`, `runId`, and `workerId` so mutation rendering cannot reduce it to a generic Ask failure.\n+\n+Successful authentication resolves the authentication incident but does not silently claim the failed turn completed. Resuming the conversation remains an explicit, owned recovery transition.\n+\n+## Named Events and Observability\n+\n+The control plane emits:\n+\n+- `account.login_required` with account, worker type, run/worker IDs when available, and a stable reason category;\n+- `account.login_started` with account and operation IDs;\n+- `account.login_awaiting_code` with account and operation IDs, but no URL or OAuth state;\n+- `account.login_verifying`;\n+- `account.login_succeeded`;\n+- `account.login_failed` with a sanitized reason;\n+- `account.login_cancelled`;\n+- `account.login_expired`;\n+- `recovery.opened` / `recovery.resolved` through the existing recovery incident contract;\n+- `error.surfaced` with `account.login_required` or a new narrowly scoped login-operation code for user-relevant failures.\n+\n+No event contains the authorization URL, authorization code, OAuth state, PKCE challenge/verifier, token, environment values, or raw PTY transcript.\n+\n+The dev event-log endpoint and headless tests must be able to establish whether login was requested, reached code entry, succeeded/failed, and resolved recovery without inspecting rendered UI.\n+\n+## UI Surface\n+\n+The existing conversation recovery notice is the primary surface. Authentication is not buried exclusively in Settings.\n+\n+The notice and login dialog use existing shadcn primitives: `Button`, `Dialog`, `Input`, and the existing error/status components. No new major screen or dashboard is introduced.\n+\n+All user-facing copy lives in every `shared/locales/*.json` file and renders through `t()`. React components subscribe with `useI18nSnapshot()`.\n+\n+Settings may also show **Sign in** for a Claude account detected as unauthenticated, using the same manager and routes. This is a secondary entry point, not a separate implementation.\n+\n+Desktop and mobile keep the primary action visible. The code field uses an appropriate text input mode, permits password-manager-neutral paste, and does not persist its value. Leaving the dialog clears the client draft after the server accepts or cancels it.\n+\n+## Persistence\n+\n+Claude owns credential storage. OmniHarness neither copies nor encrypts Claude OAuth tokens.\n+\n+OmniHarness persists only:\n+\n+- the account record and status already owned by the account subsystem;\n+- the run/worker failure and recovery incident;\n+- sanitized named lifecycle events.\n+\n+Live login operations are process-local because their PTYs cannot survive a runner restart. On restart, any previously visible nonterminal operation becomes `expired`/unavailable from the client's perspective, and the user starts a new attempt. No stale code submission may attach to a new operation.\n+\n+## Security and Trust Boundaries\n+\n+- Allow only HTTPS Anthropic/Claude authorization hosts explicitly expected from the installed Claude flow.\n+- Treat terminal output, URL text, and error text as untrusted provider output.\n+- Never render terminal output as HTML.\n+- Never accept executable/argument/environment input from the browser.\n+- Never place the authorization code in a URL, event, query cache, analytics payload, or log.\n+- Rate-limit start and code-submission attempts per authenticated OmniHarness session and account.\n+- Use constant ownership checks on runner/account/operation IDs.\n+- Do not mark the account authenticated until an account-scoped status command confirms it.\n+- Do not automatically resume a conversation after page navigation or selection changes.\n+\n+## Testing\n+\n+Implementation follows test-first development.\n+\n+Server unit and route tests cover:\n+\n+- exact authentication-failure classification;\n+- structured `account.login_required` errors and named events;\n+- fixed-command spawn and account-specific environment resolution;\n+- ANSI-stripped, chunk-split authorization URL parsing;\n+- rejection of non-HTTPS and non-Anthropic URLs;\n+- one live operation per account;\n+- code validation, non-echoing stdin delivery, and newline handling;\n+- success requiring the post-login status check;\n+- failure, cancellation, expiration, restart loss, and process cleanup;\n+- refusal of stale or mismatched operation IDs;\n+- absence of secrets from responses after initial URL delivery, events, and persisted records.\n+\n+Client manager and component tests cover:\n+\n+- browser popup opened from the direct user gesture;\n+- popup-blocked fallback link;\n+- runner/account/operation ownership guards for late responses;\n+- code draft clearing;\n+- conversation auth notice replacing the generic Ask failure;\n+- explicit resume after successful login;\n+- translated rendering for every new string.\n+\n+A lifecycle scenario drives the real HTTP/SSE control plane with a fake login executable injected only through the test harness environment, not through a production fault-injection branch. It asserts the event transcript from authentication failure through successful login and recovery resolution.\n+\n+A real browser journey would add confidence for popup behavior and focus return, but it requires explicit user approval before it is run.\n+\n+## Acceptance Criteria\n+\n+- Session `cf466dfc2492` would be described as requiring Claude sign-in, not as a generic Ask failure.\n+- The conversation remains intact and recoverable.\n+- One click starts login and opens the correct link in the normal system browser.\n+- The pasted code reaches only the matching waiting login process.\n+- Authentication is verified under the same account environment the worker will use.\n+- The UI shows deterministic running, waiting, verifying, success, cancellation, expiration, and failure states.\n+- The user can explicitly resume the conversation after success.\n+- Login and recovery decisions are visible as typed events.\n+- No OAuth secret, code, state, verifier, token, URL, or raw login transcript is persisted or logged.\n+- All relevant focused tests and lifecycle tests pass.\n+\n+## Future Direction\n+\n+The server state machine and UI manager deliberately support future automatic code delivery. A Chrome extension, Electron-controlled authorization window, or native callback relay can later submit the code through the same operation-bound endpoint. Those helpers must not change the account, recovery, security, or observability contracts defined here.\n*** End Patch";
text(await tools.apply_patch(patch));

```

Resuming the conversation remains an explicit, owned recovery transition.\n\n## Named Events and Observability\n\nThe control plane emits:\n\n- `account.login_required` with account, worker type, run/worker IDs when available, and a stable reason category;\n- `account.login_started` with account and operation IDs;\n- `account.login_awaiting_code` with account and operation IDs, but no URL or OAuth state;\n- `account.login_verifying`;\n- `account.login_succeeded`;\n- `account.login_failed` with a sanitized reason;\n- `account.login_cancelled`;\n- `account.login_expired`;\n- `recovery.opened` / `recovery.resolved` through the existing recovery incident contract;\n- `error.surfaced` with `account.login_required` or a new narrowly scoped login-operation code for user-relevant failures.\n\nNo event contains the authorization URL, authorization code, OAuth state, PKCE challenge/verifier, token, environment values, or raw PTY transcript.\n\nThe dev event-log endpoint and headless tests must be able to establish whether login was requested, reached code entry, succeeded/failed, and resolved recovery without inspecting rendered UI.\n\n## UI Surface\n\nThe existing conversation recovery notice is the primary surface. Authentication is not buried exclusively in Settings.\n\nThe notice and login dialog use existing shadcn primitives: `Button`, `Dialog`, `Input`, and the existing error/status components. No new major screen or dashboard is introduced.\n\nAll user-facing copy lives in every `shared/locales/*.json` file and renders through `t()`. React components subscribe with `useI18nSnapshot()`.\n\nSettings may also show **Sign in** for a Claude account detected as unauthenticated, using the same manager and routes. This is a secondary entry point, not a separate implementation.\n\nDesktop and mobile keep the primary action visible. The code field uses an appropriate text input mode, permits password-manager-neutral paste, and does not persist its value. Leaving the dialog clears the client draft after the server accepts or cancels it.\n\n## Persistence\n\nClaude owns credential storage. OmniHarness neither copies nor encrypts Claude OAuth tokens.\n\nOmniHarness persists only:\n\n- the account record and status already owned by the account subsystem;\n- the run/worker failure and recovery incident;\n- sanitized named lifecycle events.\n\nLive login operations are process-local because their PTYs cannot survive a runner restart. On restart, any previously visible nonterminal operation becomes `expired`/unavailable from the client's perspective, and the user starts a new attempt. No stale code submission may attach to a new operation.\n\n## Security and Trust Boundaries\n\n- Allow only HTTPS Anthropic/Claude authorization hosts explicitly expected from the installed Claude flow.\n- Treat terminal output, URL text, and error text as untrusted provider output.\n- Never render terminal output as HTML.\n- Never accept executable/argument/environment input from the browser.\n- Never place the authorization code in a URL, event, query cache, analytics payload, or log.\n- Rate-limit start and code-submission attempts per authenticated OmniHarness session and account.\n- Use constant ownership checks on runner/account/operation IDs.\n- Do not mark the account authenticated until an account-scoped status command confirms it.\n- Do not automatically resume a conversation after page navigation or selection changes.\n\n## Testing\n\nImplementation follows test-first development.\n\nServer unit and route tests cover:\n\n- exact authentication-failure classification;\n- structured `account.login_required` errors and named events;\n- fixed-command spawn and account-specific environment resolution;\n- ANSI-stripped, chunk-split authorization URL parsing;\n- rejection of non-HTTPS and non-Anthropic URLs;\n- one live operation per account;\n- code validation, non-echoing stdin delivery, and newline handling;\n- success requiring the post-login status check;\n- failure, cancellation, expiration, restart loss, and process cleanup;\n- refusal of stale or mismatched operation IDs;\n- absence of secrets from responses after initial URL delivery, events, and persisted records.\n\nClient manager and component tests cover:\n\n- browser popup opened from the direct user gesture;\n- popup-blocked fallback link;\n- runner/account/operation ownership guards for late responses;\n- code draft clearing;\n- conversation auth notice replacing the generic Ask failure;\n- explicit resume after successful login;\n- translated rendering for every new string.\n\nA lifecycle scenario drives the real HTTP/SSE control plane with a fake login executable injected only through the test harness environment, not through a production fault-injection branch. It asserts the event transcript from authentication failure through successful login and recovery resolution.\n\nA real browser journey would add confidence for popup behavior and focus return, but it requires explicit user approval before it is run.\n\n## Acceptance Criteria\n\n- Session `cf466dfc2492` would be described as requiring Claude sign-in, not as a generic Ask failure.\n- The conversation remains intact and recoverable.\n- One click starts login and opens the correct link in the normal system browser.\n- The pasted code reaches only the matching waiting login process.\n- Authentication is verified under the same account environment the worker will use.\n- The UI shows deterministic running, waiting, verifying, success, cancellation, expiration, and failure states.\n- The user can explicitly resume the conversation after success.\n- Login and recovery decisions are visible as typed events.\n- No OAuth secret, code, state, verifier, token, URL, or raw login transcript is persisted or logged.\n- All relevant focused tests and lifecycle tests pass.\n\n## Future Direction\n\nThe server state machine and UI manager deliberately support future automatic code delivery. A Chrome extension, Electron-controlled authorization window, or native callback relay can later submit the code through the same operation-bound endpoint. Those helpers must not change the account, recovery, security, or observability contracts defined here.\n?? docs/superpowers/specs/2026-08-05-claude-authentication-recovery-design.md\n"
```text
  }
]
```
